: Adjusted Rand Index and accuracy of spectral clustering on the MNIST dataset;

These experiments show that spectral clustering with 7 eigenvectors gives the best partition of the input into 10 clusters.

5.4
Future Work

This work is merely the starting point in the study of spectral clustering with fewer than
k
eigenvectors, and leaves many open questions. For example, although one can enumerate the number of used eigenvectors from
1
to
k
and take the
best clustering result with respect to some objective function (such as graph conductance or the normalised cut value),
it would be interesting to know whether the ‘optimal’ number of eigenvectors
can be computed directly.

Open Question 3
.

Can the ‘optimal’ number of eigenvectors for spectral clustering be computed directly from the Laplacian spectrum?

Additionally, more empirical studies could give further insight into the structure of the meta-graphs of real-world datasets.

Open Question 4
.

What are the typical meta-graph structures appearing in real-world datasets?
Do they share any common properties that can be exploited to improve spectral clustering in practice?

Chapter 6Finding Densely Connected Clusters Locally

In this chapter we turn our attention from learning the arbitrary structure of clusters to the problem of finding clusters with a specific known structure.
Moreover, we would like to solve the problem
locally
so that we can learn the structure of a graph close to some particular vertex of interest.

To begin, let us recall the definition of a
local clustering algorithm
.
Given an arbitrary vertex
u
of graph
{G}=({V},{E})
as input, a local graph clustering algorithm finds some low-conductance set
{S}\subset{V}
containing
u
, while the algorithm runs in time proportional to the size of the target cluster and independent of the size of the graph
{G}
.
Because of the increasing size of available datasets, which makes centralised computation too expensive, local graph clustering has become an important learning technique for analysing a number of large-scale graphs and has been applied to solve many other learning and combinatorial optimisation problems
[
ALM13
,
And10
,
AOGPT16
,
FWY20
,
LG20
,
TMIY20
,
WFH+17
,
YBLG17
]
.
In this chapter, we study local graph clustering for learning the structure of clusters that are defined by their inter-connections,
and develop local algorithms to achieve this objective in both undirected and directed graphs.
The design and analysis of the algorithms in this chapter build on the
personalised Pagerank
and
evolving set process
techniques for local clustering which are described in Section
3.3
.

The first result of this chapter is a local algorithm for finding densely connected clusters
in an undirected graph
{G}=({V},{E})
.

Main Result 4

(See Theorem
6.1
for the formal statement)
.

Given an undirected graph
{G}=({V},{E})
and a seed vertex
u
, our local algorithm finds
two
clusters
{L},{R}
around
u
, which are densely connected to each other and are loosely connected to
{V}\setminus({L}\cup{R})
.

The design of the algorithm is based on a new reduction that allows us to relate the connections between
{L},{R}
and
{V}\setminus({L}\cup{R})
to a
single
cluster in the resulting graph
{H}
, and a generalised analysis of Pagerank-based algorithms for local graph clustering.
The significance of the designed algorithm is demonstrated by experimental results on the Interstate Dispute Network from 1816 to 2010.
By connecting two vertices (countries) with an undirected edge
weighted according to the severity of their military disputes
and using the USA as the seed vertex,
the algorithm recovers two groups of countries that tend to have conflicts with each other, and shows how the two groups evolve over time.
In particular, as shown in Figure
6.1
, the algorithm not only identifies the changing roles of Russia, Japan, and eastern Europe in line with 20th century geopolitics, but also the reunification of east and west Germany around 1990.

(a)

1816-1900

(b)

1900-1950

(c)

1950-1990

(d)

1990-2010

Figure 6.1
:

Clusters found by
LocBipartDC
on the interstate dispute network, using the USA as the seed vertex. In each case, countries in the yellow cluster tend to enter conflicts with countries in the blue cluster and vice versa.

The second result of this chapter is a local algorithm for finding densely connected clusters
in a
directed graph
.

Main Result 5

(See Theorem
6.2
for the formal statement)
.

Given a directed graph
{G}=({V},{E})
and a seed vertex
u
, our local algorithm finds two clusters
{L}
and
{R}
, such that (i) there are many edges
from
{L}
to
{R}
, and (ii)
{L}\cup{R}
is loosely connected to
{V}\setminus({L}\cup{R})
.

The design of this algorithm is based on the following two techniques: (1) a
new reduction that allows us to relate the edge weight from
{L}
to
{R}
, as well as the edge connections between
{L}\cup{R}
and
{V}\setminus({L}\cup{R})
, to a
single
vertex set in the resulting
undirected
graph
{H}
; (2) a refined analysis of the ESP-based algorithm for local graph clustering.
We demonstrate that the algorithm is able to recover local densely connected clusters in the US migration dataset, in which two vertex sets
{L}
and
{R}
defined as above could represent a higher-order migration trend.
In particular, as shown in Figure
6.2
, by using different counties as starting vertices, the algorithm uncovers refined and more localised migration patterns than the previous work on the same dataset
[
CLSZ20
]
.
This algorithm is the first local clustering algorithm that achieves a similar goal on directed graphs.

(a)

Ohio Seed

(b)

New York Seed

(c)

California Seed

(d)

Florida Seed

Figure 6.2
:

Clusters found by
EvoCutDirected
in the US migration network, in which four different counties are used as starting vertices of the local algorithm.
In each case, people tend to migrate from the red counties to the green counties.

6.1
Algorithm for Undirected Graphs

We first develop and analyse a local algorithm for finding two clusters in an undirected graph with a dense cut between them.
To formalise this notion, for any undirected graph
{G}=({V},{E})
and disjoint
{L},{R}\subset{V}
, we
recall the definition of bipartiteness given in Section
3.2
:

{\beta}({L},{R})\triangleq 1-\frac{2e({L},{R})}{\mathrm{vol}({L}\cup{R})}.

Notice that a low
{\beta}({L},{R})
value means that there is a dense cut between
{L}
and
{R}
, and there is a sparse cut between

{L}\cup{R}
and
{V}\setminus({L}\cup{R})
.
In particular,
{\beta}({L},{R})=0
implies that
({L},{R})
forms a bipartite and connected component of
{G}
.
We describe a local algorithm for finding almost-bipartite sets
{L}
and
{R}
with a low value of
{\beta}({L},{R})
.

6.1.1
Reduction by Double Cover

The design of most local algorithms for finding a target set
{S}\subset{V}
of low conductance is based on analysing the behaviour of random walks starting from vertices in
{S}
. In particular, when the conductance
{\Phi}_{G}({S})
is low, a random walk starting from most vertices in
{S}
leaves
{S}
with low probability.
However, for our setting, the target is a pair of sets
{L},{R}
with many connections between them. As such, a random walk starting in either
{L}
or
{R}
is very likely to leave the starting set. To address this,
we use a novel technique based on the double cover of
{G}

to reduce the problem of finding two sets of high conductance to the problem of finding one of
low
conductance.
Formally, for any undirected graph
{G}=({V}_{G},{E}_{G})
, its
double cover
is the bipartite graph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H})
defined as follows: (1) every vertex
v\in{V}_{G}
has two corresponding vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in{V}_{H}
; (2) for every edge
\{u,v\}\in{E}_{G}
, there are edges
\{u_{1},v_{2}\}
and
\{u_{2},v_{1}\}
in
{E}_{H}
. See Figure
6.3
for an illustration.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a
b
c
d
\Longrightarrow
b_{1}
b_{2}
c_{1}
c_{2}
d_{1}
d_{2}

Figure 6.3
:
An example of the construction of the double cover

Now we show a tight connection between the value of
{\beta}({L},{R})
for any disjoint sets
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}
and
the conductance of
a
single
set in the double cover of
{G}
.
To this end, for any
{S}\subset{V}_{G}
, we define
{S}_{1}\subset{V}_{H}
and
{S}_{2}\subset{V}_{H}
by
{S}_{1}\triangleq\{v_{1}~|~v\in{S}\}
and
{S}_{2}\triangleq\{v_{2}~|~v\in{S}\}
.
We formalise the connection in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
{G}
be an undirected graph,
{S}\subset{V}
with partitioning
({L},{R})
, and
{H}
be the double cover of
{G}
.
Then, it holds that

{\Phi}_{H}({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})={\beta}_{G}({L},{R})
.

Proof.

Let
{S}^{\prime}={L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2}
, and by definition we have that
\mathrm{vol}_{G}({S})=\mathrm{vol}_{H}({S}^{\prime})
. On the other hand, it holds that

\mathrm{vol}_{H}({S}^{\prime})={w}_{H}({S}^{\prime},{V}\setminus{S}^{\prime})+2{w}_{H}({S}^{\prime},{S}^{\prime})={w}_{H}({S}^{\prime},{V}\setminus{S}^{\prime})+2{w}_{H}({L}_{1},{R}_{2}).

This implies that

{\Phi}_{H}({S}^{\prime})=\frac{{w}_{H}({S}^{\prime},{V}\setminus{S}^{\prime})}{\mathrm{vol}_{H}({S}^{\prime})}=\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{G}({S})-2{w}_{H}({L}_{1},{R}_{2})}{\mathrm{vol}_{G}({S})}=1-\frac{2{w}_{G}({L},{R})}{\mathrm{vol}_{G}({S})}={\beta}_{G}({L},{R}),

which proves the statement.
∎

Next we look at the other direction of this correspondence.
Specifically, given any
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
in the double cover of a graph
{G}
, we would like to find two disjoint sets
{L}\subset{V}_{G}
and
{R}\subset{V}_{G}
such that
{\beta}_{G}({L},{R})={\Phi}_{H}({S})
.
However, such a connection does not hold in general.
To overcome this, we restrict our attention to those subsets of
{V}_{H}
which can be unambiguously interpreted as two disjoint sets in
{V}_{G}
.

Definition 6.1
.

(Simple set)
We call
{S}\subset{V}_{H}

simple
if
\left\lvert\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\cap{S}\right\rvert\leq 1
holds for all
v\in{V}_{G}
.

Given this definition, the following corollary follows directly from Lemma
6.1
.

Corollary 6.1
.

For any simple set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
, let
{L}=\{u:u_{1}\in{S}\}
and
{R}=\{u:u_{2}\in{S}\}
. Then,

{\beta}_{G}({L},{R})={\Phi}_{H}({S}).

6.1.2
Design of the Algorithm

So far we have shown that the problem of finding densely connected sets

{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}
can be reduced to finding
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
of low conductance in the double cover
{H}
, and this reduction raises the natural question of whether existing local algorithms can be directly employed to find
{L}
and
{R}
in
{G}
.
However, this is not the case: even though a set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
returned by most local algorithms is guaranteed to have low conductance, vertices of
{G}
could be included in
{S}
twice, and as such
{S}
doesn’t necessarily give us disjoint sets
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}
with low value of
{\beta}({L},{R})
.
See Figure
6.4
for an illustration.

\cdots
a_{1}
S
b_{1}
c_{1}
d_{1}
e_{1}
f_{1}
\cdots
\cdots
a_{2}
b_{2}
c_{2}
d_{2}
e_{2}
f_{2}
\cdots

Figure 6.4
:
The indicated vertex set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}

has low conductance.
However, since
{S}
contains many pairs of vertices which correspond to the same vertex in
{G}
,
{S}
gives us little information for finding disjoint
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}
with a low
{\beta}({L},{R})
value.

The Simplify Operator

Taking the example shown in Figure
6.4
into account, our objective is to design a local algorithm for finding some set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
of low conductance which is also simple.
To ensure this, we introduce the simplify operator, and analyse its properties.

Definition 6.2

(Simplify operator)
.

Let
{H}
be the double cover of
{G}
, where the two corresponding vertices of any
u\in{V}_{G}
are defined as
u_{1},u_{2}\in{V}_{H}
. Then, for any
{\bm{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}_{\geq 0}
, the simplify operator is a function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{2n}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}_{\geq 0}
defined by

\displaystyle(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{1})

\displaystyle\triangleq\max(0,{\bm{p}}(u_{1})-{\bm{p}}(u_{2})),

\displaystyle(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq\max(0,{\bm{p}}(u_{2})-{\bm{p}}(u_{1}))

for every
u\in{V}_{G}
.

Notice that, for any vector
{\bm{p}}
and any
u\in{V}_{G}
, at most one of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
is in the support of
\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}
; hence, the support of
\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}
is always simple. To explain the meaning of
\sigma
, for a vector
{\bm{p}}
one could view
{\bm{p}}(u_{1})
as our ‘confidence’ that
u\in{L}
, and
{\bm{p}}(u_{2})
as our ‘confidence’ that
u\in{R}
. Hence, when
{\bm{p}}(u_{1})\approx{\bm{p}}(u_{2})
, both
(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{1})
and
(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{2})
are low which captures the fact that we would not prefer to include
u
in either
{L}
or
{R}
.
On the other hand, when
{\bm{p}}(u_{1})\gg{\bm{p}}(u_{2})
, we have
(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{1})\approx{\bm{p}}(u_{1})
, which captures our confidence that
u
should belong to
{L}
.
The following lemma summaries some key properties of
\sigma
.

Lemma 6.2
.

The following holds for the

\sigma
-operator:

•

\sigma\circ(c\cdot{\bm{p}})=c\cdot(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})
for
{\bm{p}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2n}
and any
c\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
;

•

\sigma\circ({\bm{a}}+{\bm{b}})\preceq\sigma\circ{\bm{a}}+\sigma\circ{\bm{b}}
for
{\bm{a}},{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2n}
;

•

\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})\preceq{\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})
for
{\bm{p}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2n}
.

Proof.

For any
u_{1}\in{V}_{H}
, we have by definition that

\displaystyle\sigma\circ(c\cdot{\bm{p}})(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\max(0,c\cdot{\bm{p}}(u_{1})-c\cdot{\bm{p}}(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=c\cdot\max(0,{\bm{p}}(u_{1})-{\bm{p}}(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=c\cdot\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}(u_{1}),

and similarly we have
\sigma\circ(c\cdot{\bm{p}})(u_{2})=c\cdot\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}(u_{2})
. Therefore, the first statement holds.

Now we prove the second statement. Let
u_{1},u_{2}\in{V}_{H}
be the vertices that correspond to
u\in{V}_{G}
. We have by definition that

\displaystyle\sigma\circ({\bm{a}}+{\bm{b}})(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\max(0,{\bm{a}}(u_{1})+{\bm{b}}(u_{1})-{\bm{a}}(u_{2})-{\bm{b}}(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=\max(0,{\bm{a}}(u_{1})-{\bm{a}}(u_{2})+{\bm{b}}(u_{1})-{\bm{b}}(u_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq\max(0,{\bm{a}}(u_{1})-{\bm{a}}(u_{2}))+\max(0,{\bm{b}}(u_{1})-{\bm{b}}(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ{\bm{a}}(u_{1})+\sigma\circ{\bm{b}}(u_{1}),

where the last inequality holds by the fact that
\max(0,x+y)\leq\max(0,x)+\max(0,y)
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.
For the same reason, we have that

\sigma\circ({\bm{a}}+{\bm{b}})(u_{2})\leq\sigma\circ{\bm{a}}(u_{2})+\sigma\circ{\bm{b}}(u_{2}).

Combining these proves the second statement.

Finally, we prove the third statement.
Recall that the lazy random walk matrix for a graph is defined to be
{\mathbf{W}}=\frac{1}{2}({\mathbf{I}}+{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{D}}^{-1})
.
By this definition, we have that

({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})=\frac{1}{2}{\bm{p}}(u_{1})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in N_{G}(u)}\frac{{\bm{p}}(v_{2})}{{d}_{H}(v_{2})},

and

({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{2})=\frac{1}{2}{\bm{p}}(u_{2})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in N_{G}(u)}\frac{{\bm{p}}(v_{1})}{{d}_{H}(v_{1})}.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})\geq({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{2})
. This implies that
(\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}}))(u_{2})=0\leq({\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}))(u_{2})
, hence it suffices to prove that
(\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}}))(u_{1})\leq({\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}))(u_{1})
. By definition, we have that

\displaystyle(\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}}))(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\max(0,({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})-({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})-({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({\bm{p}}(u_{1})-{\bm{p}}(u_{2})\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in N_{G}(u)}\left(\frac{{\bm{p}}(v_{2})}{{d}_{H}(v_{2})}-\frac{{\bm{p}}(v_{1})}{{d}_{H}(v_{1})}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({\bm{p}}(u_{1})-{\bm{p}}(u_{2})\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in N_{G}(u)}\frac{{\bm{p}}(v_{2})-{\bm{p}}(v_{1})}{{d}_{G}(v)},

(6.1)

where the last equality follows by the fact that
{d}_{H}(v_{1})={d}_{H}(v_{2})={d}_{G}(v)
for any
v\in{V}_{G}
. To analyse (
6.1
), notice that
{\bm{p}}(v_{1})-{\bm{p}}(v_{2})=(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{1})-(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{2})
for any
v\in{V}_{G}
for the following reasons:

•

When
{\bm{p}}(v_{1})\geq{\bm{p}}(v_{2})
, we have that
(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{1})-(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{2})=\left({\bm{p}}(v_{1})-{\bm{p}}(v_{2})\right)-0={\bm{p}}(v_{1})-{\bm{p}}(v_{2})
;

•

Otherwise, we have
{\bm{p}}(v_{1})<{\bm{p}}(v_{2})
and
(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{1})-(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{2})=0-({\bm{p}}(v_{2})-{\bm{p}}(v_{1}))={\bm{p}}(v_{1})-{\bm{p}}(v_{2})
.

Therefore, we can rewrite (
6.1
) as

\displaystyle(\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}}))(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}((\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{1})-(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{2}))+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in N(u)}\frac{(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{2})-(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{1})}{{d}_{G}(v)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{1})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in N(u)}\frac{(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(v_{2})}{{d}_{G}(v)}

\displaystyle=({\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}))(u_{1}),

where the inequality holds by the fact that
(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{1})\geq 0
and
(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})(u_{2})\geq 0
for any
u\in{V}_{G}
.
Since the analysis above holds for any
v\in{V}_{G}
, we have that
\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})\preceq{\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})
, which finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

While our analysis is based on all the three properties in Lemma
6.2
, the third is of particular importance: it implies that, if
{\bm{p}}
is the probability distribution of a random walk in
{H}
, applying
\sigma
before taking a one-step random walk would never result in lower probability mass than applying
\sigma
after taking a one-step random walk.
This means that no probability mass would be lost when the
\sigma
-operator is applied between every step of a random walk, in comparison with applying
\sigma
at the end of an entire random walk process.

Description of the Algorithm

Our proposed algorithm is conceptually simple: every vertex
u
of the input graph
{G}
maintains two copies
u_{1},u_{2}
of itself, and these two ‘virtual’ vertices are used to simulate
u
’s corresponding vertices in the double cover
{H}
of
{G}
.
Then, as the neighbours of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
in
{H}
are entirely determined by
u
’s neighbours in
{G}
and can be constructed locally, a random walk process in
{H}
is simulated in
{G}
.
This allows us to apply a local algorithm similar to the one by Anderson et al.
[
ACL06
]
on this ‘virtual’ graph
{H}
.
Finally, since all the required information about
u_{1},u_{2}\in{V}_{H}
is maintained by
u\in{V}_{G}
, the
\sigma
-operator can be applied locally.

The formal description of the algorithm is given in Algorithm
1
, which invokes Algorithm
2
as the key component to compute the approximate Pagerank
\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{u_{1}},{\bm{r}})
. Specifically, Algorithm
2
maintains, for every vertex
u\in{V}_{G}
, tuples
({\bm{p}}(u_{1}),{\bm{p}}(u_{2}))
and
({\bm{r}}(u_{1}),{\bm{r}}(u_{2}))
to keep track of the values of
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{r}}
in
{G}
’s double cover.
For a given vertex
u
, the entries in these tuples are expressed by
{\bm{p}}_{1}(u),{\bm{p}}_{2}(u),{\bm{r}}_{1}(u),
and
{\bm{r}}_{2}(u)
respectively.
Every
DCPush
operation (Algorithm
3
) preserves the invariant

{\bm{p}}+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}})=\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v_{1}}),

which ensures that the final output of Algorithm
2
is an approximate Pagerank vector.
It is worth noting that, although the presentation of the
ApproximatePagerankDC
procedure is similar to the one in Andersen et al.
[
ACL06
]
, in the
DCPush
procedure the update of the residual vector
{\bm{r}}
is slightly more involved: specifically, for every vertex
u\in{V}_{G}
, both
{\bm{r}}_{1}(u)
and
{\bm{r}}_{2}(u)
are needed in order to update
{\bm{r}}_{1}(u)
(or
{\bm{r}}_{2}(u)
).
That is one of the reasons that the performance of the algorithm in Andersen et al.
[
ACL06
]
cannot be directly applied for this algorithm, and a more technical analysis, some of which is parallel to theirs, is needed in order to analyse the correctness and performance of the algorithm.

Input :
A graph
{G}
, starting vertex
u
, target volume
\gamma
, and target bipartiteness
\beta

Output :
Two disjoint sets
{L}
and
{R}

Set
\alpha=\frac{\beta^{2}}{378}
, and
\epsilon=\frac{1}{20\gamma}

Compute
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}=\textsc{ApproximatePagerankDC}(u,\alpha,\epsilon)

Compute
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}^{\prime}

for

j\in[1,\left\lvert\mathrm{supp}(p)\right\rvert]

do

1

if

{\Phi}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\leq\beta

then

2

Set
{L}=\{u:u_{1}\in{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}\}
, and
{R}=\{u:u_{2}\in{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}\}

return

({L},{R})

end if

3

end for

4

Algorithm 1

LocBipartDC
({G},u,\gamma,\beta)

Input :
Starting vertex
v
, parameters
\alpha
and
\epsilon

Output :
Approximate Pagerank vector
\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v_{1}},{\bm{r}})

Set
{\bm{p}}_{1}={\bm{p}}_{2}={\bm{r}}_{2}={\bm{0}}
; set
{\bm{r}}_{1}={\bm{\chi}}_{v}

while

\max_{(u,i)\in{V}\times\{1,2\}}\frac{{\bm{r}}_{i}(u)}{{d}(u)}\geq\epsilon

do

1

Choose any
u
and
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\frac{{\bm{r}}_{i}(u)}{{d}(u)}\geq\epsilon

({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{r}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{2})=\textsc{DCPush}(\alpha,(u,i),{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{r}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{2})

end while

2

return

{\bm{p}}=\left[{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2}\right]

Algorithm 2

ApproximatePagerankDC
(v,\alpha,\epsilon)

Input :

\alpha,(u,i),{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{r}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{2}

Output :

({\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{1},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{2},{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{1},{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{2})

Set
({\bm{p}}_{1}^{\prime},{\bm{p}}_{2}^{\prime},{\bm{r}}_{1}^{\prime},{\bm{r}}_{2}^{\prime})=({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{r}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{2})

Set
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{i}(u)={\bm{p}}_{i}(u)+\alpha{\bm{r}}_{i}(u)

Set
{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{i}(u)=(1-\alpha)\frac{{\bm{r}}_{i}(u)}{2}

for

v\in N_{G}(u)

do

1

Set
{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{3-i}(v)={\bm{r}}_{3-i}(v)+(1-\alpha)\frac{{\bm{r}}_{i}(u)}{2{d}(u)}

end for

2

return

({\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{1},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{2},{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{1},{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{2})

Algorithm 3

DCPush
(\alpha,(u,i),{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{r}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{2})

6.1.3
Analysis of
ApproximatePagerankDC

We begin by considering the
ApproximatePagerankDC
algorithm, and show that it computes an approximate Pagerank vector on the double cover of the input graph.
The following lemma shows that
DCPush
maintains that throughout the algorithm
\left[{\bm{p}}_{1}^{\intercal},{\bm{p}}_{2}^{\intercal}\right]^{\intercal}
is an approximate Pagerank vector on the double cover with residual
\left[{\bm{r}}_{1}^{\intercal},{\bm{r}}_{2}^{\intercal}\right]^{\intercal}
,
where
\left[{\bm{a}}^{\intercal},{\bm{b}}^{\intercal}\right]^{\intercal}
for
{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
and
{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
is the vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}
obtained by concatenating
{\bm{a}}
and
{\bm{b}}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{1},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{2},{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{1}
and
{\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{2}
be the result of
\textsc{DCPush}((u,i),{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{r}}_{1},{\bm{r}}_{2})
. Let
{\bm{p}}=\left[{\bm{p}}_{1}^{\intercal},{\bm{p}}_{2}^{\intercal}\right]^{\intercal}
,
{\bm{r}}=\left[{\bm{r}}_{1}^{\intercal},{\bm{r}}_{2}^{\intercal}\right]^{\intercal}
,
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}=\left[({\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{1})^{\intercal},({\bm{p}}^{\prime}_{2})^{\intercal}\right]^{\intercal}
and
{\bm{r}}^{\prime}=\left[({\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{1})^{\intercal},({\bm{r}}^{\prime}_{2})^{\intercal}\right]^{\intercal}
.
Then, we have that

{\bm{p}}^{\prime}+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}}^{\prime})={\bm{p}}+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}}).

Proof.

After the push operation, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\bm{p}}+\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}}

\displaystyle{\bm{r}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\bm{r}}-{\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}}+(1-\alpha){\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\mathbf{W}}_{H}{\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}}

where
{\mathbf{W}}_{H}
is the lazy random walk matrix on the double cover of the input graph.
Then, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}})

\displaystyle={\bm{p}}+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}}-{\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}})+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}})

\displaystyle={\bm{p}}+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}}-{\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}})+\left[\alpha{\bm{r}}(u){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}}+(1-\alpha)\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}}(u){\mathbf{W}}_{H}{\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}})\right]

\displaystyle=\left[{\bm{p}}+\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}}\right]+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,\left[{\bm{r}}-{\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}}+(1-\alpha){\bm{r}}(u_{i}){\mathbf{W}}_{H}{\bm{\chi}}_{u_{i}}\right])

\displaystyle={\bm{p}}^{\prime}+\mathrm{ppr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{r}}^{\prime}).

Here, we use the fact that
\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,\cdot)
is linear and

\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}})=\alpha{\bm{s}}+(1-\alpha)\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,{\mathbf{W}}_{H}{\bm{s}}),

which follows from the fact that
\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}})=\alpha\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}(1-\alpha)^{t}{\mathbf{W}}_{H}^{t}{\bm{s}}
.
∎

Now we can show the correctness and performance guarantee of the
ApproximatePagerankDC
algorithm.

Lemma 6.4
.

For a graph
{G}
and any
v\in{V}_{G}
,
\textsc{ApproximatePagerankDC}(v,\alpha,\epsilon)
has running time
O\!\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha}\right)
and computes
{\bm{p}}=\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v_{1}},{\bm{r}})
such that
\max_{u\in{V}_{H}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}<\epsilon
and
\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})))\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha}
, where
{H}
is the double cover of
{G}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.3
, we have that
{\bm{p}}=\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v_{1}},{\bm{r}})
throughout the algorithm and so this clearly holds for the output vector.
By the stopping condition of the algorithm, it holds that
\max_{u\in{V}_{H}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}<\epsilon
.

Let
T
be the total number of push operations performed by
ApproximatePagerankDC
, and
d_{j}
be the degree of the vertex
u_{i}
used in the
j
-th push.
The amount of probability mass on
u_{i}
at time
j
is at least
\epsilon d_{j}
and so the amount of probability mass that moves from
{\bm{r}}_{i}
to
{\bm{p}}_{i}
at step
j
is at least
\alpha\epsilon d_{i}
.
Since
\left\|{\bm{r}}_{1}\right\|_{1}+\left\|{\bm{r}}_{2}\right\|_{1}=1
at the beginning of the algorithm, we have that

\alpha\epsilon\sum_{j=1}^{T}d_{j}\leq 1,

which implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{T}d_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha\epsilon}.

(6.2)

Now, note that for every vertex in
\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})
, there must be at least one push operation on that vertex. So,

\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}}))=\sum_{v\in\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})}{d}(v)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{T}d_{j}\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha}.

Finally, to bound the running time, we can implement the algorithm by maintaining a queue of vertices satisfying
\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}\geq\epsilon
, and repeat the push operation on the first item in the queue at every iteration, updating the queue as appropriate.
The push operation and queue updates can be performed in time proportional to
{d}(u)
and so the running time follows from (
6.2
).
∎

6.1.4
Analysis of
LocBipartDC

We now come to the analysis of the main algorithm and the core technical result of this chapter.
To prove the correctness of the
LocBipartDC
algorithm, we show two complementary facts which we state informally here:

1.

If there is a simple set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
with low conductance, then for most
u_{1}\in{S}
the simplified approximate Pagerank vector
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{u_{1}},{\bm{r}})
would have a lot of probability mass on a small set of vertices.

2.

If
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{u_{1}},{\bm{r}})
contains a lot of probability mass on some small set of vertices, then there is a sweep set of
{\bm{p}}
with low conductance.

As we see in Section
6.1.1
, there is a direct correspondence between almost-bipartite sets in
{G}
, and low-conductance and simple sets in
{H}
.
This means that the two facts above are exactly what we need to prove that
Algorithm
1

can find densely connected sets in
{G}
.

We first show in Lemma
6.5
how the
\sigma
-operator affects some standard mixing properties of Pagerank vectors in order to establish the first fact promised above.
This lemma relies on the fact that
{S}\subset{V}_{G}
corresponds to a
simple
set in
{V}_{H}
.
This allows us to apply the
\sigma
-operator to the approximate Pagerank vector
\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{u_{1}},{\bm{r}})
while preserving a large probability mass on the target set.
We make use of the following fact proved by Andersen et al.
[
ACL06
]
which shows that the approximate Pagerank vector has a large probability mass on a set of vertices if they have low conductance.

Proposition 6.1

(
[
ACL06
]
, Theorem 4)
.

For any vertex set
{S}
and any constant
\alpha\in[0,1]
, there is a subset
{S}_{\alpha}\subseteq{S}
with
\mathrm{vol}({S}_{\alpha})\geq\mathrm{vol}({S})/2
such that for any vertex
v\in{S}_{\alpha}
, the approximate Pagerank vector
\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},r)
satisfies

\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({S})\geq 1-\frac{{\Phi}({S})}{\alpha}-\mathrm{vol}({S})\max_{u\in{V}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}.

The following lemma generalises this result to show that applying the
\sigma
-operator to the approximate Pagerank vector maintains this large probability mass up to a constant factor.

Lemma 6.5
.

For any set
{S}\subset{V}_{{G}}
with partitioning
({L},{R})
and any constant
\alpha\in[0,1]
, there is a subset
{S}_{\alpha}\subseteq{S}
with
\mathrm{vol}({S}_{\alpha})\geq\mathrm{vol}({S})/2
such that, for any vertex
v\in{S}_{\alpha}
, the simplified approximate Pagerank on the double cover
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\left(\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v_{1}},{\bm{r}})\right)
satisfies

{\bm{p}}({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})\geq 1-\frac{2{\beta}({L},{R})}{\alpha}-2\mathrm{vol}({S})\max_{u\in{V}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.1
and Proposition
6.1
, there is a set
{S}_{\alpha}\subseteq{E}
with
\mathrm{vol}({S}_{\alpha})\geq\mathrm{vol}({S})/2
such that it holds for
v\in{S}_{\alpha}
that

\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})\geq 1-\frac{{\beta}({L},{R})}{\alpha}-\mathrm{vol}({S})\max_{u\in{V}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}.

Therefore, by the definition of
{\bm{p}}
we have that

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})-\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({L}_{2}\cup{R}_{1})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})-\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})(\overline{{L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2}})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})-(1-\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2}))

\displaystyle=2\cdot\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})-1

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{2{\beta}({L},{R})}{\alpha}-2\mathrm{vol}({S})\max_{u\in{V}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)},

which proves the statement of the lemma.
∎

To prove the second fact promised at the beginning of this subsection, we show as an intermediate lemma that the value of
{\bm{p}}(u_{1})
can be bounded with respect to its value after taking a step of the random walk:
({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})
.
In order to prove this, we need to carefully analyse the effect of the
\sigma
-operator on the approximate Pagerank vector, and in particular examine the effect of the residual vector
{\bm{r}}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
{G}
be a graph with double cover
{H}
, and
\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})
be the approximate Pagerank vector defined with respect to
{H}
. Then,
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\left(\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})\right)
satisfies that

{\bm{p}}(u_{1})\leq\alpha\left({\bm{s}}(u_{1})+{\bm{r}}(u_{2})\right)+(1-\alpha)({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})
, and
{\bm{p}}(u_{2})\leq\alpha\left({\bm{s}}(u_{2})+{\bm{r}}(u_{1})\right)+(1-\alpha)({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{2})

for any
u\in{V}_{G}
.

Proof.

Let
u\in{V}_{G}
be an arbitrary vertex. We have that

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\big(\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}})-\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,{\bm{r}})\big)(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\big(\alpha{\bm{s}}+(1-\alpha){\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}})-\alpha{\bm{r}}-(1-\alpha){\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{ppr}(\alpha,{\bm{r}})\big)(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\big(\alpha{\bm{s}}+(1-\alpha){\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})-\alpha{\bm{r}}\big)(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\max(0,(\alpha{\bm{s}}+(1-\alpha){\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}}))(u_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\quad{}-(\alpha{\bm{s}}+(1-\alpha){\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}}))(u_{2})+\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{2})-\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\circ\big(\alpha{\bm{s}}+(1-\alpha){\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})\big)(u_{1})+\max(0,\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{2})-\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\circ\big(\alpha{\bm{s}}+(1-\alpha){\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})\big)(u_{1})+\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\circ\big(\alpha{\bm{s}}\big)(u_{1})+(1-\alpha)\sigma\circ\big({\mathbf{W}}\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})\big)(u_{1})+\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha{\bm{s}}(u_{1})+\alpha{\bm{r}}(u_{2})+(1-\alpha)({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1}),

where the first equality follows by the definition of the approximate Pagerank, the second equality follows by the definition of the Pagerank, and the last two inequalities follow by Lemma
6.2
. This proves the first statement, and the second statement follows by symmetry.
∎

Notice that applying the
\sigma
-operator for any vertex
u_{1}
introduces a new dependency on the value of the residual vector
{\bm{r}}
at
u_{2}
.
This subtle observation demonstrates the additional complexity introduced by the
\sigma
-operator when compared with previous analysis of Pagerank-based local algorithms
[
ACL06
]
.
Taking account of the
\sigma
-operator, let us further analyse the Lovász-Simonovits curve defined by
{\bm{p}}
, which is a common technique in the analysis of random walks on graphs
[
LS90
]
.
Our goal is to show that if there is a set
{S}
with a large value of
{\bm{p}}({S})
, then there must be a sweep set
{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}
with low conductance.
We first bound the curve at some point by the conductance of the corresponding sweep set.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\left(\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})\right)
. It holds for any
j\in[n-1]
that

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right]\leq

\displaystyle\alpha\Big({\bm{s}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right]+{\bm{r}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right]\Big)

\displaystyle{}+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{2}\Big({\bm{p}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})-\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert\right]\Big)

\displaystyle{}+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{2}\Big({\bm{p}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})+\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert\right]\Big).

Since
\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\pm\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert=\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})(1\pm{\Phi}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}))
, this tells us that when the conductance of the sweep set
{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}
is high, the Lovász-Simonovits curve around the point
\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})
is close to a straight line.
For the proof of Lemma
6.7
, we view the undirected graph
{H}
instead as a directed graph, and each edge
\{u,v\}\in{E}_{H}
as a pair of directed edges
(u,v)
and
(v,u)
. For any edge
(u,v)
and vector
{\bm{p}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2n}
, let

p(u,v)=\frac{{\bm{p}}(u)}{{d}(u)}

and for a set of directed edges
{A}
, let

p({A})=\sum_{(u,v)\in{A}}p(u,v).

Now for any set of vertices
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
, let
\mathrm{in}({S})=\{(u,v)\in{E}_{H}:v\in{S}\}
and
\mathrm{out}({S})=\{(u,v)\in{V}_{H}:u\in{S}\}
.
Additionally, for any simple set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
, we define the
simple complement
of the set
{S}
by

\widehat{{S}}=\{u_{2}:u_{1}\in{S}\}\cup\{u_{1}:u_{2}\in{S}\}.

We need the following property of the Lovász-Simonovitz curve with regard to sets of edges.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any set of edges
{A}
, it holds that

p({A})\leq{\bm{p}}\!\left[\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert\right].

Proof.

For each
u\in{V}
, let
x_{u}=\left\lvert\{(u,v)\in{A}\}\right\rvert
be the number of edges in
{A}
with the tail at
u
.
Then, we have by definition that

\displaystyle p({A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(u,v)\in{A}}\frac{{\bm{p}}(u)}{{d}(u)}=\sum_{u\in{V}}\frac{x_{u}}{{d}(u)}{\bm{p}}(u).

Since
x_{u}/{d}(u)\in[0,1]
and

\sum_{u\in{V}}\frac{x_{u}}{{d}(u)}{d}(u)=\sum_{u\in{V}}x_{u}=\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert,

the statement follows from (
3.1
).
∎

We also make use of the following lemma from
[
ACL06
]
.

Lemma 6.9

(
[
ACL06
]
, Lemma 4)
.

For any distribution
{\bm{p}}
and any set of vertices
{A}
,

({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})({A})\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(p(\mathrm{in}({A})\cup\mathrm{out}({A}))+p(\mathrm{in}({A})\cap\mathrm{out}({A}))\right).

These two facts give us everything we need to prove the behaviour of the Lovász-Simonovits curve described in Lemma
6.7
.

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

By definition, we have that

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}({S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{u_{1}\in{S}}{\bm{p}}(u_{1})+\sum_{u_{2}\in{S}}{\bm{p}}(u_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u_{1}\in{S}}\Big(\alpha\left({\bm{s}}(u_{1})+{\bm{r}}(u_{2})\right)+(1-\alpha)({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad{}+\sum_{u_{2}\in{S}}\Big(\alpha\left({\bm{s}}(u_{2})+{\bm{r}}(u_{1})\right)+(1-\alpha)({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot\Bigg(\sum_{u_{1}\in{S}}{\bm{s}}(u_{1})+\sum_{u_{2}\in{S}}{\bm{s}}(u_{2})+\sum_{u_{1}\in{S}}{\bm{r}}(u_{2})+\sum_{u_{2}\in{S}}{\bm{r}}(u_{1})\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\quad{}+(1-\alpha)\cdot\Bigg(\sum_{u_{1}\in{S}}({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{1})+\sum_{u_{2}\in{S}}({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})(u_{2})\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot\Big({\bm{s}}({S})+{\bm{r}}\left(\widehat{{S}}\right)\!\Big)+(1-\alpha)\cdot({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})({S})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\Big({\bm{s}}({S})+{\bm{r}}\left(\widehat{{S}}\right)\!\Big)+(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Big(p(\mathrm{in}(S)\cup\mathrm{out}(S))+p(\mathrm{in}(S)\cap\mathrm{out}(S))\Big),

where the first inequality follows by Lemma
6.6
and the last one follows by Lemma
6.9
.

Now, recall that
{\bm{p}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right]={\bm{p}}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})
and notice that
\mathrm{vol}\left(\widehat{{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}}\right)=\mathrm{vol}\left({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}\right)
holds by the definition of
\widehat{{S}}
. Hence, using Lemma
6.8
, we have that

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right]

\displaystyle={\bm{p}}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left({\bm{s}}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})+{\bm{r}}\left(\widehat{{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}}\right)\right)+(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\Big(p\left(\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cup\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right)+p(\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cap\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}))\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\Big({\bm{s}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right]+{\bm{r}}\!\left[\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right]\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}+(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\left({\bm{p}}\!\left[\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cup\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert\right]+{\bm{p}}\!\left[\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cap\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert\right]\right).

It remains to show that

\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cap\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert=\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})-\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert,

and

\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cup\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert=\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})+\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert.

The first follows by noticing that
\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cap\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert
counts precisely twice the number of edges with both endpoints inside
{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}
.
The second follows since

\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cup\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert=\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cap\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\setminus\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathrm{out}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\setminus\mathrm{in}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert

and both the second and third terms on the right hand side are equal to
\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert
.
∎

We now show another technical lemma which bounds the Lovász-Simonovits curve with respect to the conductance of the sweep sets
{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}
.
To understand this lemma,
recall from the discussion in Section
3.3
that the Lovász-Simonovits curve of the stationary distribution is linear:
{\bm{\pi}}[k]=k/\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})
.
This lemma bounds how far the Lovász-Simonovits curve deviates from
that of the stationary distribution.
In particular, if there is no sweep set with low conductance, then the deviation must be small.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\left(\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})\right)
such that
\max_{u\in{V}_{H}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}\leq\epsilon
, and
\phi
be any constant in
[0,1]
. If
{\Phi}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\geq\phi
for all
j\in[\left\lvert\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})\right\rvert]
, then

{\bm{p}}[k]-\frac{k}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\leq\alpha t+\alpha\epsilon kt+\sqrt{\min(k,\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})-k)}\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}

for all
k\in[0,\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})]
and integer
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

For ease of discussion we write
k_{j}=\mathrm{vol}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})
and let
\overline{k_{j}}=\min(k_{j},\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})-k_{j})
. For convenience we write

f_{t}(k)=\alpha t+\alpha\epsilon kt+\sqrt{\min(k,\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})-k)}\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}.

We prove by induction that for all
t\geq 0
,

{\bm{p}}[k]-\frac{k}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\leq f_{t}(k).

For the base case, this holds for
t=0
for any
\phi
. Our proof is by case distinction:
(1) for any integer
k\in[\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})-1]
,

{\bm{p}}[k]-\frac{k}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\leq 1\leq\sqrt{\min(k,\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})-k)}\leq f_{0}(k);

(2)
for
k=0
or
k=\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})
,
{\bm{p}}[k]-\frac{k}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}=0\leq f_{0}(k)
. The base case follows, since
f_{0}
is concave and
{\bm{p}}[k]
is linear between integer values.

Now for the inductive case, we assume that the claim holds for
t
and show that it holds for
t+1
.
It suffices to show that the following holds for every
j\in[\left\lvert\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})\right\rvert]
:

{\bm{p}}[k_{j}]-\frac{k_{j}}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\leq f_{t+1}(k_{j}).

The lemma follows because this inequality holds trivially for
j=0
and
j=n
and
{\bm{p}}[k]
is linear between
k_{j}
for
j\in\{0,n\}\cup[1,\left\lvert\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})\right\rvert]
.

For any
j\in[1,\left\lvert\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})\right\rvert]
, it holds by Lemma
6.7
that

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left({\bm{s}}\!\left[k_{j}\right]+{\bm{r}}\!\left[k_{j}\right]\right)+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{2}\left({\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}-\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert\right]+{\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}+\left\lvert\partial({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\right\rvert\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\left({\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}-{\Phi}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cdot\overline{k_{j}}\right]+{\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}+{\Phi}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\cdot\overline{k_{j}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\left({\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}-\phi\overline{k_{j}}\right]+{\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}+\phi\overline{k_{j}}\right]\right),

where the second inequality uses the fact that
{\bm{r}}\!\left[k_{j}\right]\leq\epsilon k_{j}
and the last inequality uses the concavity of
{\bm{p}}\!\left[k\right]
and the fact that
{\Phi}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})\geq\phi
. By the induction hypothesis, we have that

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\left(f_{t}(k_{j}-\phi\overline{k_{j}})+\frac{k_{j}-\phi\overline{k_{j}}}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}+f_{t}(k_{j}+\phi\overline{k_{j}})+\frac{k_{j}+\phi\overline{k_{j}}}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}+\frac{k_{j}}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}+\frac{1}{2}\left(f_{t}(k_{j}+\phi\overline{k_{j}})+f_{t}(k_{j}-\phi\overline{k_{j}})\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}+\alpha t+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}t+\frac{k_{j}}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg(\sqrt{\min(k_{j}-\phi\overline{k_{j}},\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})-k_{j}+\phi\overline{k_{j}})}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\sqrt{\min(k_{j}+\phi\overline{k_{j}},\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})-k_{j}-\phi\overline{k_{j}})}\Bigg)\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(t+1)+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}(t+1)+\frac{k_{j}}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\overline{k_{j}}-\phi\overline{k_{j}}}+\sqrt{\overline{k_{j}}+\phi\overline{k_{j}}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}

Now, using the Taylor series of
\sqrt{1+\phi}
at
\phi=0
, we see that for any
x\geq 0
and
\phi\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{x-\phi x}+\sqrt{x+\phi x}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{x}}{2}\left(\left(1-\frac{\phi}{2}-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}-\frac{\phi^{3}}{16}\right)+\left(1+\frac{\phi}{2}-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}+\frac{\phi^{3}}{16}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{x}\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right).

Applying this with
x=\overline{k_{j}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}\!\left[k_{j}\right]-\frac{k_{j}}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(t+1)+\alpha\epsilon k_{j}(t+1)+\sqrt{\overline{k_{j}}}\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle=f_{t+1}(k_{j}),

which completes the proof.
∎

We can now use this bound to show the other direction: if the Lovász-Simonovits curve is particularly large at some point, then there must be a sweep set with low conductance.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
{G}
be a graph with double cover
{H}
, and let
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\left(\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})\right)
such that
\max_{u\in{V}_{H}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(u)}{{d}(u)}\leq\epsilon
.
If there is a set of vertices
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
and a constant
\delta
such that

{\bm{p}}({S})-\frac{\mathrm{vol}({S})}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\geq\delta
,
then there is some
j\in[1,\left\lvert\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})\right\rvert]
such that

{\Phi}_{H}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})<6\sqrt{(1+\epsilon\mathrm{vol}({S}))\alpha\ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right)\big/\delta}.

Proof.

Let
{H}_{c}
be a weighted version of the double cover with the weight of every edge equal to some constant
c
.
Let
k=\mathrm{vol}_{{H}_{c}}({S})
and
\overline{k}=\min(k,\mathrm{vol}({V}_{{H}_{c}})-k)
.
Notice that the behaviour of random walks and the conductance of vertex sets are unchanged by re-weighting the graph by a constant factor.
As such,
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\mathrm{apr}_{{H}_{c}}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})
and we let
\phi=\min_{j}{\Phi}_{{H}_{c}}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})
. Hence, by Lemma
6.10
it holds for any
t>0
that

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle<{\bm{p}}\!\left[k\right]-\frac{k}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{{H}_{c}})}<\alpha t(1+\epsilon k)+\sqrt{\overline{k}}\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}.

Letting
v=\mathrm{vol}({V}_{{H}_{c}})
we set

t=\left\lceil\frac{8}{\phi^{2}}\ln\frac{2\sqrt{v/2}}{\delta}\right\rceil
.
Now, assuming that

\delta\geq\frac{2}{\sqrt{v/2}}

(6.3)

we have

\delta<(1+\epsilon k)\alpha\left\lceil\frac{8}{\phi^{2}}\ln\frac{2\sqrt{v/2}}{\delta}\right\rceil+\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt{v/2}}\sqrt{\overline{k}}<\frac{9(1+\epsilon k)\alpha\ln(v/2)}{\phi^{2}}+\frac{\delta}{2}.

We set
c
such that

v=c\ \mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})=\mathrm{vol}({V}_{{H}_{c}})=\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right)^{2},

which satisfies (
6.3
) and implies that

\phi<\sqrt{\frac{36(1+\epsilon k)\alpha\ln(4/\delta)}{\delta}}.

By the definition of
\phi
, there must be some
{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}
with the desired property.
∎

We have now shown the two facts promised at the beginning of this subsection.
Putting these together,
if there is a simple set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
with low conductance, then we can find a sweep set of
\sigma\circ\mathrm{apr}(\alpha,{\bm{s}},{\bm{r}})
with low conductance.
By the reduction from almost-bipartite sets in
{G}
to low-conductance simple sets in
{H}
, our target set corresponds to a simple set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
which leads to Algorithm
1
for finding almost-bipartite sets. Our result is summarised as follows.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
{G}
be an
n
-vertex undirected graph, and
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}
be disjoint sets such that
{\beta}({L},{R})\leq\beta
and
\mathrm{vol}({L}\cup{R})\leq\gamma
. Then, there is a set
{C}\subseteq{L}\cup{R}
with
\mathrm{vol}(C)\geq\mathrm{vol}({L}\cup{R})/2
such that, for any
v\in{C}
,
\textsc{LocBipartDC}\left({G},v,\gamma,\sqrt{7560\beta}\right)
returns
({L}^{\prime},{R}^{\prime})
with
{\beta}({L}^{\prime},{R}^{\prime})=O\!\left(\sqrt{\beta}\right)
and
{\mathrm{vol}({L}^{\prime}\cup{R}^{\prime})}=O\!\left(\beta^{-1}\gamma\right)
.
Moreover, the algorithm has running time
O\!\left(\beta^{-1}\gamma\log n\right)
.

Proof.

We set the parameters of Algorithm
1
to be

•

\alpha=\widehat{\beta}^{2}/378=20\beta
, and

•

\epsilon=1/(20\gamma)
.

Let
{\bm{p}}=\sigma\circ\left(\mathrm{apr}_{H}(\alpha,{\bm{\chi}}_{v},{\bm{r}})\right)
be the simplified approximate Pagerank vector computed in the algorithm, which satisfies that
\max_{v\in{V}_{H}}\frac{{\bm{r}}(v)}{{d}(v)}\leq\epsilon
, and
{C}^{\prime}={L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2}
be the target set in the double cover. Hence, by Lemma
6.5
we have that

{\bm{p}}({C}^{\prime})\geq 1-\frac{2\beta}{\alpha}-2\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{vol}({C})\geq 1-\frac{1}{10}-\frac{1}{10}=\frac{4}{5}.

Notice that, since
{C}^{\prime}
is simple, we have that
\frac{\mathrm{vol}({C}^{\prime})}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and

{\bm{p}}({C}^{\prime})-\frac{\mathrm{vol}({C}^{\prime})}{\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}\geq\frac{4}{5}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{3}{10}.

Therefore, by Lemma
6.11
and choosing
\delta=3/10
, there is some
j\in[1,\left\lvert\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}})\right\rvert]
such that

\displaystyle{\Phi}({S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}})

\displaystyle<\sqrt{\frac{360}{3}(1+\epsilon\mathrm{vol}({C}))\alpha\ln\left(\frac{40}{3}\right)}\leq\sqrt{\left(1+\frac{1}{20}\right)7200\beta}=\widehat{\beta}.

Since such a
{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}
is a simple set, by Lemma
6.1
, we know that the sets
{L}^{\prime}=\{u\in{V}_{G}:u_{1}\in{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}\}
and
{R}^{\prime}=\{u\in{V}_{G}:u_{2}\in{S}_{j}^{{\bm{p}}}\}
satisfy
{\beta}({L}^{\prime},{R}^{\prime})\leq\widehat{\beta}
.
For the volume guarantee, Lemma
6.4
gives that

\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}}))\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha}=\frac{\gamma}{\beta}.

Since the sweep set procedure in the algorithm is over the support of
{\bm{p}}
, we have that

\mathrm{vol}({L}^{\prime}\cup{R}^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}}))\leq\beta^{-1}\gamma.

Now, we analyse the running time of the algorithm.
By Lemma
6.4
, computing the approximate Pagerank vector
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
takes time

O\!\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha}\right)=O\!\left(\beta^{-1}\gamma\right).

Since
\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}}^{\prime}))=O\!\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha}\right)
, computing
\sigma\circ\left({\bm{p}}^{\prime}\right)
can also be done in time
O\!\left(\beta^{-1}\gamma\right)
.
The cost of checking each conductance in the sweep set loop is
O\!\left(\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{supp}({\bm{p}}))\log(n)\right)=O\!\left(\beta^{-1}\gamma\log(n)\right)
. This dominates the running time of the algorithm, which completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

We remark that the quadratic approximation guarantee in Theorem
6.1
matches the state-of-the-art local algorithm for finding a single set with low conductance
[
AOGPT16
]
.
Furthermore,
this result presents a significant improvement over the previous state-of-the-art by Li and Peng
[
LP13
]
, whose design is based on an entirely different technique.
For any
\epsilon\in[0,1/2]
, their algorithm has running time
O\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\beta^{-2}\gamma^{1+\epsilon}\log^{3}\gamma\right)
and returns a set with volume
O\!\left(\gamma^{1+\epsilon}\right)
and bipartiteness
O\!\left(\sqrt{\beta/\epsilon}\right)
.
In particular, their algorithm requires much higher time complexity in order to guarantee the same bipartiteness
O\left(\sqrt{\beta}\right)
.

6.2
Algorithm for Directed Graphs

We now turn our attention to local algorithms for finding densely-connected clusters in directed graphs.
In comparison with undirected graphs, we are interested in finding disjoint
{L},{R}\subset{V}
of an input directed graph
{G}=({V},{E})
such that most of the edges adjacent to
{L}\cup{R}
are
from
{L}
to
{R}
. To formalise this,
we define the
flow ratio
from
{L}
to
{R}
as

F({L},{R})\triangleq 1-\frac{2{w}({L},{R})}{\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{out}}({L})+\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{in}}({R})},

where
{w}({L},{R})
is the number of directed edges from
{L}
to
{R}
. Notice that we take not only edge densities but also edge directions into account:
a low
F({L},{R})
-value tells us that almost all edges with their tail in
{L}
have their head in
{R}
, and conversely almost all edges with their head in
{R}
have their tail in
{L}
. One could also see
F({L},{R})
as a generalisation of
{\beta}({L},{R})
.
In particular, if we view an undirected graph as a directed graph by replacing each edge with two directed edges, then

{\beta}({L},{R})=F({L},{R})
. In this section, we develop a local algorithm for finding such vertex sets in a directed graph, and analyse the algorithm’s performance.

6.2.1
Reduction by Semi-Double Cover

Given a directed graph
{G}=({V}_{G},{E}_{G})
, we construct its
semi-double cover

{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H})
as follows:
(1) every vertex
v\in{V}_{G}
has two corresponding vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in{V}_{H}
; (2) for every edge
(u,v)\in{E}_{G}
, we add the edge
\{u_{1},v_{2}\}
in
{E}_{H}
, see Figure
6.5
for illustration.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a
b
c
d
\Longrightarrow
b_{1}
b_{2}
c_{1}
c_{2}
d_{1}
d_{2}

Figure 6.5
:
An example of a directed graph and its semi-double cover

It is worth comparing this reduction with the one for undirected graphs:

•

For undirected graphs, we apply the standard double cover and every undirected edge in
{G}
corresponds to two edges in the double cover
{H}
;

•

For directed graphs, every directed edge in
{G}
corresponds to
one
undirected edge in
{H}
. This
asymmetry
would allow us to ‘recover’ the direction of any edge in
{G}
.

We follow the use of
{S}_{1},{S}_{2}
from Section
6.1
:
for any
{S}\subset{V}_{G}
,
we define
{S}_{1}\subset{V}_{H}
and
{S}_{2}\subset{V}_{H}
by
{S}_{1}\triangleq\{v_{1}~|~v\in{S}\}
and
{S}_{2}\triangleq\{v_{2}~|~v\in{S}\}
.
The lemma below shows the connection between the value of
F_{G}({L},{R})
for any
{L},{R}
and
{\Phi}_{H}({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
{G}
be a directed graph with semi-double cover
{H}
. Then, it holds for any
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}

that

F_{G}({L},{R})={\Phi}_{H}({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})
.
Similarly, for any simple set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
, let
{L}=\{u:u_{1}\in{S}\}
and
{R}=\{u:u_{2}\in{S}\}
. Then, it holds that
F_{G}({L},{R})={\Phi}_{H}({S})
.

Proof.

We define
{S}_{H}={L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2}
, and have that

\displaystyle{\Phi}_{H}({S}_{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{{w}({S}_{H},{V}_{H}\setminus{S}_{H})}{\mathrm{vol}_{H}({S}_{H})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{H}({S}_{H})-2{w}_{H}({S}_{H},{S}_{H})}{\mathrm{vol}_{H}({S}_{H})}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2{w}_{H}({S}_{H},{S}_{H})}{\mathrm{vol}_{H}({S}_{H})}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2{w}_{G}({L},{R})}{\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{out}}({L})+\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{in}}({R})}

\displaystyle=F_{G}({L},{R}).

This proves the first statement of the lemma.
The second statement of the lemma follows by the same argument.
∎

6.2.2
Design and Analysis of the Algorithm

The new algorithm is a modification of the algorithm by Andersen and Peres
[
AP09
]
described in Section
3.3.2
.
Given a directed graph
{G}
as input, the algorithm simulates the volume-biased ESP on
{G}
’s semi-double cover
{H}
.
Notice that the graph
{H}
can be constructed locally in the same way as the local construction of the double cover.
However, as the output set
{S}
of an ESP-based algorithm is not necessarily simple, the algorithm only returns vertices
u\in{V}_{G}
in which
exactly
one of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are included in
{S}
.
The key procedure for the algorithm is given in Algorithm
4
, in which the
GenerateSample
procedure is the one described at the end of Section
3.3.2
.

Input :
Starting vertex
u
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, target flow ratio
\phi

Output :
A pair of sets
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}

Set
T=\lfloor(100\phi^{\frac{2}{3}})^{-1}\rfloor

Compute
{S}=\textsc{GenerateSample}_{H}(u_{i},T)

Let
{L}=\{u\in{V}_{G}:u_{1}\in{S}
and
u_{2}\not\in{S}\}

Let
{R}=\{u\in{V}_{G}:u_{2}\in{S}
and
u_{1}\not\in{S}\}

return

{L}
and
{R}

Algorithm 4

EvoCutDirected
(u,i,\phi)

Notice that in the constructed graph
{H}
,

{\Phi}({L}_{1}\cup{R}_{2})\leq\phi
does not imply that
{\Phi}({L}_{2}\cup{R}_{1})\leq\phi
.
Due to this asymmetry, Algorithm
4
takes a parameter
i\in\{1,2\}
to indicate whether the starting vertex is in
{L}
or
{R}
.
If it is not known whether
u
is in
{L}
or
{R}
,
two copies
can be run in parallel, one with
i=1
and the other with
i=2
.
Once one of them terminates with the performance guaranteed in Theorem
6.2
, the other
can be terminated.
Hence, we can always assume that it is known whether the starting vertex
u
is in
{L}
or
{R}
.

We now analyse the algorithm. Notice that, since the evolving set process gives us an arbitrary set on the semi-double cover, Algorithm
4
converts this into a simple set by
removing any vertices
u
where
u_{1}\in{S}
and
u_{2}\in{S}
. The following definition allows us to discuss sets which are close to being simple.

Definition 6.3

(
\epsilon
-simple set)
.

For any set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
, let
{P}=\{u_{1},u_{2}:u\in{V}_{G},u_{1}\in{S}~\mbox{and}~u_{2}\in{S}\}
. We call set
{S}

\epsilon
-simple if it holds that

\frac{\mathrm{vol}({P})}{\mathrm{vol}({S})}\leq\epsilon.

The notion of
\epsilon
-simple sets measures the ratio of vertices
in which both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are in
{S}
.
In particular, any simple set defined in Definition
6.1
is
0
-simple.
We show that, if
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
is an
\epsilon
-simple set for small
\epsilon
, we can construct a simple set
{S}^{\prime}
with low conductance.

Lemma 6.13
.

For any
\epsilon
-simple set
{S}\subset{V}_{H}
,
let
{P}=\{u_{1},u_{2}:u\in{V}_{G},u_{1}\in{S}\mbox{ and }u_{2}\in{S}\}

and set
{S}^{\prime}={S}\setminus{P}
.
Then,

{\Phi}({S}^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}({\Phi}({S})+\epsilon).

Proof.

By the definition of conductance, we have

\displaystyle{\Phi}({S}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{{w}({S}^{\prime},{V}\setminus{S}^{\prime})}{\mathrm{vol}({S}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{w}({S},{V}\setminus{S})+\mathrm{vol}({P})}{\mathrm{vol}({S})-\mathrm{vol}({P})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\frac{{w}({S},{V}\setminus{S})+\mathrm{vol}({P})}{\mathrm{vol}({S})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left(\frac{{w}({S},{V}\setminus{S})}{\mathrm{vol}({S})}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left({\Phi}({S})+\epsilon\right),

which proves the lemma.
∎

Given this, in order to guarantee that
{\Phi}({S}^{\prime})
is low, we need to construct
{S}
such that
{\Phi}({S})
is low and
{S}
is
\epsilon
-simple for small
\epsilon
.
The following lemma shows that, by choosing the number of steps of the evolving set process carefully, we can very tightly control the overlap of the output of the evolving set process with the target set
{S}
, which we know to be simple.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
{C}\subset{V}_{H}
be a set with conductance
{\Phi}({C})=\phi
, and let
T=(100\phi^{\frac{2}{3}})^{-1}
. There exists a set
{C}_{g}\subseteq{C}
with volume at least
\mathrm{vol}({C})/2
such that for any
x\in{C}_{g}
, with probability at least
7/9
, a sample path
({S}_{0},{S}_{1},\ldots,{S}_{T})
from the volume-biased ESP started from
{S}_{0}=\{x\}
satisfies the following:

•

{\Phi}({S}_{t})<60\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\sqrt{\ln(\mathrm{vol}({V}))}
for some
t\in[0,T]
;

•

\mathrm{vol}({S}_{t}\cap{C})\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{10}\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\mathrm{vol}({S}_{t})
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.3
, with probability at least
(1-1/9)
there is some
t<T
such that

\displaystyle{\Phi}({S}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 3\sqrt{4T^{-1}\ln\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}=3\sqrt{400\phi^{\frac{2}{3}}\ln\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H})}=60\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\sqrt{\ln(\mathrm{vol}({V}_{H}))}.

Then, by Proposition
3.1
we have that

\mathrm{esc}(x,T,{C})\leq T\phi=\frac{1}{100}\phi^{1/3}
.
Hence, by Proposition
3.4
, with probability at least
9/10
,

\max_{t\leq T}\frac{\mathrm{vol}({S}_{t}\setminus{C})}{\mathrm{vol}({S}_{t})}\leq 10\cdot\mathrm{esc}(x,T,{C})\leq\frac{1}{10}\cdot\phi^{1/3}

Applying the union bound on these two events proves the statement of the lemma.
∎

This allows us to analyse the performance of Algorithm
4
, which
is summarised in Theorem
6.2
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
{G}
be an
n
-vertex directed graph, and
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{G}
be disjoint sets such that
F({L},{R})\leq\phi
and
\mathrm{vol}({L}\cup{R})\leq\gamma
. There is a set
{C}\subseteq{L}\cup{R}
with
\mathrm{vol}({C})\geq\mathrm{vol}({L}\cup{R})/2
such that, for any
v\in{C}
and some
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\textsc{EvoCutDirected}({G},v,i,\phi)
returns
({L}^{\prime},{R}^{\prime})
such that
F({L}^{\prime},{R}^{\prime})=O\!\left(\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}n\right)
and
{\mathrm{vol}({L}^{\prime}\cup{R}^{\prime})}=O\!\left(\left(1-\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)^{-1}\gamma\right)
.
Moreover, the algorithm has running time
O\!\left(\phi^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gamma\log^{\frac{3}{2}}n\right)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.14
, we know that, with probability at least
7/9
, the set
{S}
computed by Algorithm
4
satisfies

\mathrm{vol}({S}\cap{C})\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{10}\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\mathrm{vol}({S}).

Let
{P}=\{u_{1},u_{2}:u_{1}\in{S}
and
u_{2}\in{S}\}
. Then, since
{C}
is simple, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{vol}({P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot(\mathrm{vol}({S})-\mathrm{vol}({S}\cap{C}))\leq 2\cdot\left(1-\left(1-\frac{1}{10}\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\right)\cdot\mathrm{vol}({S})=\frac{1}{5}\cdot\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\mathrm{vol}({S}),

which implies that
{S}

is
(1/5)\cdot\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}
-simple.
Therefore, by letting
{S}^{\prime}={S}\setminus{P}
, we have by Lemma
6.13
that

\displaystyle{\Phi}({S}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\cdot\left({\Phi}({S})+\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{5}}\left({\Phi}({S})+\frac{1}{5}\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\leq\frac{5}{4}\cdot{\Phi}({S})+\frac{1}{4}\cdot\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}.

Since
{\Phi}({S})=O\!\left(\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\ln^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)\right)
by Lemma
6.14
, we have that
{\Phi}({S}^{\prime})=O\!\left(\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}\ln^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)\right)
.
For the volume guarantee, direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{vol}({S}^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{vol}({S})\leq\frac{\mathrm{vol}({S}\cap{C})}{1-\frac{1}{10}\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{vol}({C})}{1-\phi^{\frac{1}{3}}}.

Finally, the running time of the algorithm is dominated by the
GenerateSample
method, which is shown in
[
AP09
]
to have running time
O\!\left(\mathrm{vol}({C})\phi^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln^{\frac{3}{2}}(n)\right)
.
∎

Algorithm
4
is the first local algorithm for directed graphs that approximates a pair of densely connected clusters,
and demonstrates that finding such a pair appears to be much easier than finding a low-conductance set in a directed graph; in particular, existing local algorithms for finding a low-conductance set require the stationary distribution of the random walk in the directed graph
[
ACL07
]
, the sublinear-time computation of which is unknown
[
CKP+17
]
.
However, the knowledge of the stationary distribution is not needed for our algorithm.

6.3
Experimental Results

In this section we evaluate the performance of the newly proposed algorithms on both synthetic and real-world datasets.
For undirected graphs, we compare the performance of
LocBipartDC
against the previous state-of-the-art
[
LP13
]
through synthetic datasets with various parameters, and apply a real-world dataset to demonstrate the significance of the new algorithm.
For directed graphs, we compare the performance of
EvoCutDirected
with the state-of-the-art
non-local
algorithm since our presented local algorithm for directed graphs is the first such algorithm in the literature.
All experiments are performed on a Lenovo Yoga 2 Pro with an Intel(R) Core(TM) i7-4510U CPU @ 2.00GHz processor and 8GB of RAM.
The code can be downloaded from

https://github.com/pmacg/local-densely-connected-clusters
.

6.3.1
Results for Undirected Graphs

We first compare the performance of
LocBipartDC
with the previous state-of-the-art given by Li and Peng
[
LP13
]
, which we can refer to as
LP
, on synthetic graphs generated from the stochastic block model (SBM).
Specifically, we assume that the graph has
k=3
clusters
\{{C}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3}
, and the number of vertices in each cluster, denoted by
n_{1},n_{2}
and
n_{3}
respectively, satisfy
n_{1}=n_{2}=0.1n_{3}
. Moreover, any pair of vertices
u\in{C}_{i}
and
v\in{C}_{j}
is connected with probability
P_{i,j}
.
We assume that
P_{1,1}=P_{2,2}=p_{1}
,
P_{3,3}=p_{2}
,
P_{1,2}=q_{1}
, and
P_{1,3}=P_{2,3}=q_{2}
.
Throughout the experiments, we maintain the ratios
p_{2}=2p_{1}
and
q_{2}=0.1p_{1}
, leaving the parameters
n_{1}
,
p_{1}
and
q_{1}
free.
Notice that the different values of
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
guarantee that
{C}_{1}
and
{C}_{2}
are the ones optimising the
\beta
-value, which is why the proposed model is slightly more involved than the standard SBM described in Section
2.3.2
.

We evaluate the quality of the output
({L},{R})
returned by each algorithm with respect to its
\beta
-value, the Adjusted Rand Index (ARI)
[
GA17
]
, as well as the ratio of the misclassified vertices defined by
\left(\left\lvert{L}\triangle{C}_{1}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{R}\triangle{C}_{2}\right\rvert\right)/\left(\left\lvert{L}\cup{C}_{1}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{R}\cup{C}_{2}\right\rvert\right)
.
All the reported results are the average performance of each algorithm over
10
runs, in which a random vertex from
{C}_{1}\cup{C}_{2}
is chosen as the starting vertex of the algorithm.

Setting the
\epsilon_{LP}
parameter in the
LP
algorithm.

The
LP
algorithm has an additional parameter over
LocBipartDC
, which we refer to as
\epsilon_{LP}
.
This parameter influences the runtime and performance of the algorithm and must be in the range
[0,1/2]
.
In order to choose a fair value of
\epsilon_{LP}
for comparison with
LocBipartDC
, we consider several values on graphs with a range of target volumes.

We generate graphs from the SBM such that
p_{1}=1/n_{1}
and
q_{1}=100p_{1}
and vary the size of the target set by varying
n_{1}
between
100
and
10,000
.
For values of
\epsilon_{LP}
in
\{0.01,0.02,0.1,0.5\}
, Figure
6.6
(a) shows how the runtime of the
LP
algorithm compares to the
LocBipartDC
algorithm for a range of target volumes.
In this experiment, the runtime of the
LocBipartDC
algorithm lies between the runtimes of the
LP
algorithm for
\epsilon_{LP}=0.01
and
\epsilon_{LP}=0.02
.
However, Figure
6.6
(b) shows that the performance of the
LP
algorithm with
\epsilon_{LP}=0.01
is significantly worse than the performance of the
LocBipartDC
algorithm, and so for a fair comparison we set
\epsilon_{LP}=0.02
for the remainder of the experiments.

(a)

(b)

:
(a)

LocBipartDC
algorithm and the
LP
algorithm with various values of
\epsilon_{LP}
.

(b)
Setting
\epsilon_{LP}=0.01
results in a significantly worse Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) than the

algorithm.

We now compare the
LocBipartDC
and
LP
algorithms’
performance on graphs generated from the SBM with different values of
n_{1},p_{1}
and
q_{1}
.
As shown in Table
6.1
,
LocBipartDC
not only runs faster, but also produces better clusters with respect to all three metrics.
Secondly, since the clustering task becomes more challenging when the target clusters have higher
\beta
-value,
we compare the algorithms’ performance on a sequence of instances with increasing value of
\beta
.
Since
q_{1}/p_{1}=2(1-\beta)/\beta
, we simply fix the values of
n_{1},p_{1}
as
n_{1}=1,000,p_{1}=0.001
, and generate graphs with increasing value of
q_{1}/p_{1}
; this gives us graphs with monotone values of
\beta
.
As shown in Figure
6.7
(a),
the performance of our algorithm is always better than the previous state-of-the-art.

Thirdly, notice that both algorithms use some parameters to control the algorithm’s runtime and the output’s approximation ratio, which are naturally influenced by each other. To study this dependency, we generate graphs according to
n_{1}=100,000
,
p_{1}=0.000015
, and
q_{1}=0.00027
which results in target sets with
\beta\approx 0.1
and volume
\gamma\approx 6,000,000
.
Figure
6.7
(b) shows that, in comparison with the previous state-of-the-art,
LocBipartDC
takes much less time to produce output with the same ARI value.

(a)

(b)

:

-value. A higher
q_{1}/p_{1}
ratio corresponds to a lower
\beta
-value.

Experiments on the real-world dataset.

We further demonstrate the significance of our algorithm on the Interstate Disputes Dataset (v3.1)
[
MJK+19
]
, which
records every
interstate dispute during 1816–2010, including the level of hostility resulting from the dispute and the number of casualties, and has been widely studied in the social and political sciences
[
MMR02
,
MMT08
]
as well as the machine learning community
[
HRC17
,
ME11
,
TB09
]
.
For a given time period, we construct a graph from the data by representing each country with a vertex and adding an edge between each pair of countries weighted according to the severity of any military disputes between those countries.
Specifically, if there’s a war
1
1

1

A war is defined by the maintainers of the dataset as a series of battles resulting in at least 1,000 deaths.

between the two countries, the corresponding two vertices are
connected by an edge with weight
30
; for any other dispute that is not part of an interstate war, the two corresponding vertices are connected by an edge with weight
1
.
We always use the USA as the starting vertex of the algorithm, and the algorithm’s output, as visualised in Figure
6.1
, can be well explained by geopolitics. The

\beta
-values of the pairs of clusters in Figures
6.1
are
0.361
,
0.356
,
0.170
and
0.191
respectively.

6.3.2
Results for Directed Graphs

Next we evaluate the performance of our algorithm for directed graphs on synthetic and real-world datasets.
Since there are no previous local directed graph clustering algorithms that achieve similar objectives, we compare the output of Algorithm
4
(
ECD
) with the state-of-the-art
non-local algorithm proposed by Cucuringu et al.
[
CLSZ20
]
, and we refer this to as
CLSZ
in the following.

Synthetic dataset.

Let us first look at the
cyclic block model
(CBM) described in Cucuringu et al.
[
CLSZ20
]
with parameters
k
,
n
,
p
,
q
, and
\eta
.
In this model, we generate a directed graph with
k
clusters
{C}_{1},\ldots,{C}_{k}
of size
n
, and for
u,v\in{C}_{i}
, there is an edge between
u
and
v
with probability
p
and the edge direction is chosen uniformly at random.
For
u\in{C}_{i}
and
v\in{C}_{i+1\mod k}
, there is an edge between
u
and
v
with probability
q
, and the edge is directed from
u
to
v
with probability
\eta
and from
v
to
u
with probability
1-\eta
.
Throughout our experiments, we fix
p=0.001
,
q=0.01
, and
\eta=0.9
.

Secondly, since the goal of our algorithm is to find local structure in a graph, we extend the cyclic block model with additional local clusters and refer to this model as CBM+.
In addition to the parameters of the CBM, we introduce the parameters
n^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\eta^{\prime}
.
In this model, the clusters
{C}_{1}
to
{C}_{k}
are generated as in the CBM, and there are two additional clusters
{C}_{k+1}
and
{C}_{k+2}
of size
n^{\prime}
.
For
u,v\in{C}_{k+i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, there is an edge between
u
and
v
with probability
p

and for
u\in{C}_{k+1}
and
v\in{C}_{k+2}
, there is an edge with probability
q^{\prime}_{1}
;
the edge directions are chosen uniformly at random.
For
u\in{C}_{k+1}\cup{C}_{k+2}
and
v\in{C}_{1}
, there is an edge with probability
q^{\prime}_{2}
.
If
u\in{C}_{k+1}
, the orientation is from
v
to
u
with probability
\eta^{\prime}
and from
u
to
v
with probability
1-\eta^{\prime}
and if
u\in{C}_{k+2}
, the orientation is from
u
to
v
with probability
\eta^{\prime}
and from
v
to
u
with probability
1-\eta^{\prime}
.
We always fix
q^{\prime}_{1}=0.5
,
q^{\prime}_{2}=0.005
, and
\eta^{\prime}=1
.
Notice that the clusters
{C}_{k+1}
and
{C}_{k+2}
form a ‘local’ cycle with the cluster
{C}_{1}
.

Table
6.2
reports the average performance over 10 runs with a variety of parameters.
We can see that
CLSZ
uncovers the global structure in the CBM more accurately than
ECD
.
On the other hand,
CLSZ
fails to identify the local cycle in the CBM+ model, while
ECD
succeeds.

on synthetic data.

Real-world dataset.

Now we evaluate the algorithms’ performance
on the US Migration Dataset
[
CB
]
.
For fair comparison, we follow Cucuringu et al.
[
CLSZ20
]
and construct the directed graph as follows: every county in the mainland USA is represented by a vertex; for any vertices
j,\ell
, the edge weight of
(j,\ell)
is given by

\left|\frac{{\mathbf{M}}_{j,\ell}-{\mathbf{M}}_{\ell,j}}{{\mathbf{M}}_{j,\ell}+{\mathbf{M}}_{\ell,j}}\right|,

where
{\mathbf{M}}_{j,\ell}
is the number of people who migrated from county
j
to county
\ell
between 1995 and 2000; in addition, the direction of
(j,\ell)
is set to be from
j
to
\ell
if
{\mathbf{M}}_{j,\ell}>{\mathbf{M}}_{\ell,j}
, otherwise the direction is set to be the opposite.

For
CLSZ
, we follow their suggestion on the same dataset and set
k=10
.
Both algorithms’ performance is evaluated with respect to the flow ratio, as well as the cut imbalance ratio used in their work.
For any vertex sets
{L}
and
{R}
, the
cut imbalance ratio
is defined by

\mathrm{CI}({L},{R})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left|\frac{{w}({L},{R})-{w}({R},{L})}{{w}({L},{R})+{w}({R},{L})}\right|,

and a higher
\mathrm{CI}({L},{R})
value indicates the connection between
{L}
and
{R}
is more significant. Using counties in Ohio, New York, California, and Florida as the starting vertices, the outputs of
EvoCutDirected
are visualised in Figures
6.2
,
and in Table
6.3
we compare them
to the top
4
pairs returned by
CLSZ
, which are shown in Figure
6.8
.
The new algorithm produces better outputs with respect to the two metrics.

These experiments suggest that local algorithms are not only more efficient, but can also be much more effective than global algorithms when learning certain structures in graphs. In particular, some localised structure
might be hidden when applying the objective function over the entire graph.

6.4
Future Work

This chapter presents a novel graph reduction technique, and applies this to design two local algorithms for learning a certain structure of clusters in both undirected and directed graphs. Our experimental results further demonstrate their potential wide applications in learning meaningful structures of real-world datasets. Our work leaves several open questions for future work.

Firstly, there is a gap between the approximation guarantee of the algorithm for directed graphs when compared with undirected graphs, and it looks very challenging to close this gap with the techniques presented in this chapter.
To understand this, it is insightful to discuss why Algorithm
1
cannot be applied for directed graphs, although the input graph is translated into an
undirected
graph by the semi-double cover reduction.
This is because, when translating a directed graph into a bipartite undirected graph, the third property of the
\sigma
-operator in Lemma
6.2
no longer holds, since the existence of the edge
\{u_{1},v_{2}\}\in{E}_{H}
does not necessarily imply that
\{u_{2},v_{1}\}\in{E}_{H}
.
Indeed, Figure
6.9
gives a counterexample in which
\left(\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}})\right)(u)\not\leq\left({\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}})\right)(u)
.

a_{1}
