Figure 6.9
:
Consider the
directed graph and its semi-double cover above. Suppose
{\bm{p}}(a_{1})={\bm{p}}(a_{2})=0.5
and
{\bm{p}}(b_{1})={\bm{p}}(b_{2})=0
. It is straightforward to check that
(\sigma\circ({\mathbf{W}}{\bm{p}}))(b_{2})=0.25
and
({\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\circ{\bm{p}}))(b_{2})=0
.

This means that the typical analysis of a Pagerank vector with the Lovász-Simonovitz curve cannot be applied anymore.
In addition, it is interesting to note that one cannot apply the tighter analysis of the ESP process given by Andersen et al.
[
AOGPT16
]
to the new algorithm in this chapter.
The key to their analysis is an
improved bound on the probability that a random walk escapes from the target cluster.
This bound given a better guarantee for the conductance of the output set at the cost of a relaxed guarantee on the overlap between the output set and the target set.
Since the analysis for directed graphs in this chapter relies on a very tight guarantee on the overlap of the output set with the target set, we cannot use their improvement in this setting.
These difficulties leave the following important open question.

Open Question 5
.

Suppose we are given a directed graph
{G}=({V},{E})
containing clusters
{L},{R}\subset{V}
with
F({L},{R})=\phi
.
Is there a local algorithm which is guaranteed to return
{L}^{\prime},{R}^{\prime}\subset{V}
with
F({L}^{\prime},{R}^{\prime})=\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\phi})
?

Secondly, although this chapter shows that two densely connected clusters can be approximately recovered with local algorithms, it would be very interesting to know whether our presented technique can be generalised to find clusters whose structure is defined by some meta-graph as described in Chapter
5
.

Open Question 6
.

Suppose we are given a graph
{G}=({V},{E})
, and
{S}_{1},\ldots,{S}_{k}\subset{V}

are clusters defined with respect to some meta-graph.
Is there a local algorithm that approximately recovers these
{S}_{1},\ldots,{S}_{k}
?

Chapter 7Finding Bipartite Components in Hypergraphs

Hypergraphs are important objects to model ternary or higher-order relations of objects, and have a number of applications in analysing many complex datasets occurring in practice.
In this chapter we study a new heat diffusion process in hypergraphs, and employ this process to design a polynomial-time algorithm that approximately finds bipartite components in a hypergraph.
We theoretically study the performance of the proposed algorithm, and compare it against the previous state-of-the-art through extensive experimental analysis on both synthetic and real-world datasets.
We see that the new algorithm consistently and significantly outperforms the previous state-of-the-art across a wide range of hypergraphs.
The problem studied in this chapter can be seen as a generalisation of the problem studied in the previous chapter, although we study algorithms in the global setting rather than the local one.

A unifying theme of this thesis is the design of efficient algorithms based on spectral methods, which study the graph Laplacian matrix in particular.
The results of the present chapter form part of a sequence of work to generalise many of the results of spectral graph theory to hypergraphs.
We introduce and study the non-linear Laplacian-type operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
for any hypergraph
{H}
.
While we formally define the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
in Section
7.3
, one can informally think about
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
as a variant of the non-linear hypergraph Laplacian
{\mathbf{L}}_{H}
studied in
[
CLTZ18
,
LM18
,
TMIY20
]
which generalises the
signless Laplacian operator
of graphs.
We develop a polynomial-time algorithm that finds an eigenvalue
\lambda
and its associated eigenvector of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
.

Main Result 6

(See Theorem
7.2
for the formal statement)
.

Given a hypergraph
{H}
, there is a non-linear operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
which generalises the signless graph Laplacian operator for hypergraphs. Moreover, there
is a polynomial-time algorithm that finds an eigenvalue of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
along with its associated eigenvector.

The algorithm is based on the following heat diffusion process:
starting from an arbitrary vector
{\bm{f}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that describes the initial heat distribution among the vertices, we use
{\bm{f}}_{0}
to construct some graph
1
1

1

Note that the word ‘graph’ always refers to a non-hyper graph. Similarly, we always use
{\mathbf{L}}_{H}
to refer to the
non-linear
hypergraph Laplacian operator, and use
{\mathbf{L}}_{G}
as the standard graph Laplacian.

{G}_{0}
, and use the diffusion process in
{G}_{0}
to represent the one in the original hypergraph
{H}
and update
{\bm{f}}_{t}
; this process continues until the time at which
{G}_{0}
cannot be used to appropriately simulate the diffusion process in
{H}
any more.
At this point, we use the currently maintained
{\bm{f}}_{t}
to construct another graph
{G}_{t}
to simulate the diffusion process in
{H}
, and update
{\bm{f}}_{t}
.
This process continues until the vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
converges; see Figure
7.1
for illustration.
We prove that this heat diffusion process is unique, well-defined, and the maintained vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
converges to an eigenvector of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
.

Figure 7.1
:
Illustration of the proposed diffusion process. In each time step, we construct a graph
{G}
based on the current vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
, and update
{\bm{f}}_{t}
with the
{\mathbf{J}}_{G}
operator.
Notice that the graph

{\bm{f}}_{t}

{H}
into two well-connected sets (all the edges are adjacent to both sets), by splitting according to positive and negative entries.

The second result of this chapter is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
as input,
finds disjoint subsets
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{H}
that are highly connected with each other.

Main Result 7

(See Theorem
7.1
for the formal statement)
.

Suppose we are given a hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
, and an eigenvalue
\lambda
of the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
with its corresponding eigenvector.
There is an algorithm that finds two clusters
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{H}
such that
{\beta}_{H}({L},{R})\leq\sqrt{2\lambda}
, where
{\beta}_{H}
is the
hypergraph bipartiteness
.

The key to this algorithm is a Cheeger-type inequality for hypergraphs that relates the spectrum of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
and the
hypergraph bipartiteness
of
{H}
, an analogue of the graph bipartiteness studied in Chapter
6
.
Both the design and analysis of the algorithm is inspired by Trevisan
[
Tre12
]
, however the analysis is much more involved because of the non-linear operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
and hyperedges of different ranks.
This result alone answers an open question posed by Yoshida
[
Yos19
]
, who asks whether there is a hypergraph operator which satisfies a Cheeger-type inequality for bipartiteness.

The significance of our algorithmic result is demonstrated by extensive experimental studies of the algorithm on both synthetic and real-world datasets.
In particular, on the well-known Penn Treebank corpus that contains 49,208 sentences and over 1 million words,
our
purely unsupervised
algorithm is able to identify a significant fraction of verbs from non-verbs in its two output clusters.
Hence, this work could potentially have many applications in unsupervised learning for hypergraphs.

Finally, we study the spectrum of the non-linear Laplacian-type operators for hypergraphs, and show that, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there is a hypergraph on
n
vertices such that
{\mathbf{L}}_{H}
and
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
have more than
n
eigenvectors.
This answers an open question posed by Chan et al.
[
CLTZ18
]
, who ask whether
{\mathbf{L}}_{H}
could have more than
2
eigenvectors
2
2

2

Notice that, while the operator
{\mathbf{L}}_{G}
of a graph
{G}
has
n
eigenvalues, the number of eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{L}}_{H}
is unknown because of its non-linearity.
.

7.1
Additional Notation

In order to study densely connected clusters in hypergraphs, we need some new notation and definitions.
Suppose, we have a hypergraph
{H}=({V},{E},{w})
, then for any
{A},{B}\subset{V}
we can overload the weight function to define the cut value between
{A}
and
{B}
by

{w}({A},{B})\triangleq\sum_{e\in{E}}{w}(e)\cdot\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{e\cap{A}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap{B}\neq\emptyset}\right.}e\cap{A}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap{B}\neq\emptyset\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{e\cap{A}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap{B}\neq\emptyset}\right]}.

Sometimes, we would like to analyse the weights of edges that intersect some vertex sets and not others. To this end, for any
{A},{B},{C}\subseteq{V}
, let

{w}({A},{B}~|~{C})\triangleq\sum_{e\in{E}}{w}(e)\cdot\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{e\cap{A}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap{B}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap C=\emptyset}\right.}e\cap{A}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap{B}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap C=\emptyset\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{e\cap{A}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap{B}\neq\emptyset\land e\cap C=\emptyset}\right]},

and we can also define
{w}({A}~|~{C})\triangleq{w}({A},{A}~|~{C})
for simplicity.
Generalising the notion of the bipartiteness of a graph, the
hypergraph bipartiteness
of sets
{L},{R}\subset{V}
is defined by

{\beta}_{H}({L},{R})\triangleq\frac{2{w}({L}|\overline{{L}})+2{w}({R}|\overline{{R}})+{w}({L},\overline{{L}}|{R})+w({R},\overline{{R}}|{L})}{\mathrm{vol}({L}\cup{R})},

and we define

{\beta}_{H}\triangleq\min_{{S}\subset{V}_{H}}{\beta}_{H}({S},{V}_{H}\setminus{S}).

This definition generalises the graph bipartiteness (Definition
3.3
) in that, if every edge in
{H}
contains
2
vertices, then
{H}
is a (non-hyper) graph and
{\beta}_{H}({L},{R})
is equivalent to the graph bipartiteness.
Informally, a low value of
{\beta}_{H}({L},{R})
indicates that most of the edges which intersect either
{L}
or
{R}
connect
{L}
and
{R}
together.
The goal of this chapter is to develop an algorithm to find such sets
{L}
and
{R}
in a hypergraph.

For any hypergraph
H
and
{\bm{f}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define the
discrepancy
of an edge
e\in{E}_{H}
with respect to
{\bm{f}}
as

{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\triangleq\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v).

Moreover, the weighted discrepancy of
e\in{E}_{H}
is

c_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\triangleq{w}(e)\left\lvert{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\right\rvert,

and for any set
{S}\subseteq{E}_{H}
we have

c_{{\bm{f}}}({S})\triangleq\sum_{e\in{S}}c_{{\bm{f}}}(e).

The discrepancy of
{H}
with respect to
{\bm{f}}
is defined by

D({\bm{f}})\triangleq\frac{\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}{w}(e)\cdot{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)^{2}}{\sum_{v\in{V}_{H}}{d}_{H}(v)\cdot{\bm{f}}(v)^{2}}.

For any

{\bm{f}},{\bm{g}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the weighted inner product between
{\bm{f}}
and
{\bm{g}}
is defined by

\left\langle{\bm{f}},{\bm{g}}\right\rangle_{w}\triangleq{\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{g}},

where
{\mathbf{D}}_{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the diagonal matrix consisting of the degrees of all the vertices of
{H}
. The weighted norm of
{\bm{f}}
is defined by
\left\|{\bm{f}}\right\|_{w}^{2}\triangleq\left\langle{\bm{f}},{\bm{f}}\right\rangle_{w}
.

For any non-linear operator
{\mathbf{J}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we say that
(\lambda,{\bm{f}})
is an eigen-pair if and only if
{\mathbf{J}}{\bm{f}}=\lambda{\bm{f}}
. Note that
in contrast with linear operators, a non-linear operator does not necessarily have
n
eigenvalues.
Additionally, we define the weighted Rayleigh quotient of the operator
{\mathbf{J}}
and vector
{\bm{f}}
to be

R_{\mathbf{J}}({\bm{f}})\triangleq\frac{{\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}{\bm{f}}}{\left\|{\bm{f}}\right\|_{w}^{2}}.

It is important to remember that throughout this chapter the letter
{H}
is always used to represent a hypergraph, and
{G}
to represent a graph.

7.2
Baseline Algorithms

Before we present the new algorithm, let us first consider the baseline algorithms for finding bipartite components in hypergraphs.
One natural idea is to
construct a graph
{G}
from the original hypergraph
{H}
and apply a graph algorithm on
{G}
to find a good approximation of the cut structure of
{H}

[
CR19
,
LM17
,
ZHS06
]
.
However, most graph reductions would introduce a factor of
r
, which relates to the rank of hyperedges in
{H}
, into the approximation guarantee.
To see this, consider the following two natural graph reductions:

1.

Random Reduction
: From
{H}=({V},{E}_{H})
, we construct
{G}=({V},{E}_{G})
in which every hyperedge
e\in{E}_{H}
is replaced by an edge connecting two randomly chosen vertices in
e
;

2.

Clique Reduction
: From
{H}=({V},{E}_{H})
, we construct
{G}=({V},{E}_{G})
in which
every hyperedge
e\in{E}_{H}
is replaced by a clique of
\mathrm{rank}(e)
vertices in
{G}
.

To discuss the drawback of both reductions, we study the following two
r
-uniform hypergraphs
{H}_{1}
and
{H}_{2}
, in each of which the vertex set is
{L}\cup{R}
and the edge set is defined as follows:

1.

in
{H}_{1}
, every edge contains exactly one vertex from
{L}
, and
r-1
vertices from
{R}
;

2.

in
{H}_{2}
, every edge contains exactly
r/2
vertices from
{L}
and
r/2
vertices from
{R}
.

As such, we have that
{w}_{{H}_{1}}({L},{R})=\left\lvert{E}_{{H}_{1}}\right\rvert
, and
{w}_{{H}_{2}}({L},{R})=\left\lvert{E}_{{H}_{2}}\right\rvert
.
See Figure
7.2
for illustration.

{L}
{R}
Hypergraph
{H}_{1}
{L}
{R}
Hypergraph
{H}_{2}
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots

Figure 7.2
:
Hypergraphs illustrating the downside of simple reductions

Now we analyse the size of the cut
({L},{R})
in the reduced graphs.

•

Let
{w}_{{G}}({L},{R})
be the cut value of
({L},{R})
in the
random
graph
{G}
constructed from
{H}
by the random reduction. We have for
{H}_{1}
that
\mathbb{E}[{w}_{{G}}({L},{R})]=\Theta(1/r)\cdot{w}_{{H}_{1}}({L},{R})
and for
{H}_{2}
that
\mathbb{E}[{w}_{{G}}({L},{R})]=\Theta(1)\cdot{w}_{{H}_{2}}({L},{R})
.

•

Similarly, by setting
{w}_{{G}}({L},{R})
to be the cut value of
({L},{R})
in the reduced graph
{G}
constructed from
{H}
by the clique reduction, we have for
{H}_{1}
that

{w}_{{G}}({L},{R})=\Theta(r)\cdot{w}_{{H}_{1}}({L},{R})

and for
{H}_{2}
that

{w}_{{G}}({L},{R})=\Theta(r^{2})\cdot{w}_{{H}_{2}}({L},{R})
.

Notice that these two approximation ratios differ by a factor of
r
in the two reductions.
This suggests that reducing an
r
-uniform hypergraph
{H}
to a graph with some simple reduction would always introduce a factor of
r
into the approximation guarantee.
This is one of the main reasons to develop spectral theory for hypergraphs through heat diffusion processes
[
CLTZ18
,
TMIY20
,
Yos19
]
.

7.3
Diffusion Process and Algorithm

In this section, we introduce a new diffusion process in hypergraphs, and use it to design a polynomial-time algorithm for finding bipartite components in hypergraphs.
We first study the heat diffusion process in graphs to give some intuition.
Then, we generalise it to hypergraphs and describe the new algorithm.
Finally, we sketch some of the analysis which proves that the diffusion process is well defined.
The full analysis is given in Sections
7.4
and
7.5
.

7.3.1
Diffusion Process in Graphs

To discuss the intuition behind our designed diffusion process, let us look at the case of graphs. Let
{G}=({V},{E},{w})
be a graph, and we have for any
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that

\frac{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{G}{\bm{x}}}{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}}=\frac{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}({\mathbf{I}}+{\mathbf{D}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{G}{\mathbf{A}}_{G}{\mathbf{D}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{G}){\bm{x}}}{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}}.

By setting
{\bm{x}}={\mathbf{D}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{G}{\bm{y}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{G}{\bm{x}}}{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bm{y}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{G}{\mathbf{Z}}_{G}{\mathbf{D}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{G}{\bm{y}}}{{\bm{y}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{G}{\bm{y}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bm{y}}^{\intercal}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}+{\mathbf{A}}_{G}){\bm{y}}}{{\bm{y}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{G}{\bm{y}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{E}_{G}}{w}(u,v)\cdot({\bm{y}}(u)+{\bm{y}}(v))^{2}}{\sum_{u\in{V}_{G}}{d}_{G}(u)\cdot{\bm{y}}(u)^{2}}.

(7.1)

It is easy to see that
\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{Z}}_{G})=0
if
{G}
is bipartite, and it is known that
\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{Z}}_{G})
and its corresponding eigenvector
{\bm{f}}_{1}({\mathbf{Z}}_{G})
are closely related to two densely connected components of
{G}

[
Tre12
]
.
Moreover, similar to the heat equation for graph Laplacian
{\mathbf{L}}_{G}
, suppose
{\mathbf{D}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is some measure on the vertices of
{G}
, then a diffusion process defined by the differential equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-{\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t}\

(7.2)

converges to the eigenvector of
{\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}
with minimum eigenvalue and
can be employed to find two densely connected components of the underlying graph.
3
3

3

For the reader familiar with the heat diffusion process of graphs (e.g.,
[
Chu07
,
KG14
]
), notice that the above-defined process essentially employs the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{G}
to
replace the Laplacian
{\mathbf{L}}_{G}
when defining the heat diffusion: through
{\mathbf{J}}_{G}
, the heat diffusion can be used to find two densely connected components of
{G}
.

7.3.2
Hypergraph Diffusion and our Algorithm

Now we study whether one can construct a new hypergraph operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
that generalises the diffusion in graphs to hypergraphs.
First of all, we focus on a fixed time
t
with measure vector
{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We follow (
7.2
) and define the rate of change

\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}

so that the diffusion can proceed for an infinitesimal time step.
Our intuition is that the rate of change due to some edge
e\in{E}_{H}
should involve only the vertices in
e
with the maximum or minimum value in the normalised measure
{\bm{f}}_{t}
.
To formalise this, for any edge
e\in{E}_{H}
we define

{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\triangleq\{v\in e:{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)=\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)\}\mbox{\ \ \ and\ \ \ }{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\triangleq\{v\in e:{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)=\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)\}.

That is, for any edge
e
and normalised measure
{\bm{f}}_{t}
,
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\subseteq e
consists of the vertices
v
adjacent to
e
whose
{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)
values are maximum, and
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\subseteq e
consists of the vertices
v
adjacent to
e
whose
{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)
values are minimum.
See Figure
7.3
for an example.
Then, applying the
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
operator to a vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
should be equivalent to applying the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{G}
for some graph
{G}
which we construct by splitting the weight of each hyperedge
e\in{E}_{H}
between the edges in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\times{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
.
Similar to the case for graphs and (
7.3.1
), for any
{\bm{x}}={\mathbf{D}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}
this gives us the quadratic form

\displaystyle\frac{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{H}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\mathbf{D}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{H}{\bm{x}}}{{\bm{x}}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}}=\frac{{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t}}{{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{E}_{G}}{w}_{G}(u,v)\cdot({\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+{\bm{f}}_{t}(v))^{2}}{\sum_{u\in{V}_{G}}{d}_{H}(u)\cdot{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}{w}_{H}(e)(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(v))^{2}}{\sum_{u\in{V}_{H}}{d}_{H}(u)\cdot{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)^{2}},

where
{w}_{G}(u,v)
is the weight of the edge
\{u,v\}
in
{G}
, and
{w}_{H}(e)
is the weight of the edge
e
in
{H}
.
We see later in this chapter that
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
has an eigenvalue of
0
if the hypergraph is
2
-colourable
4
4

4

Hypergraph
{H}
is
2
-colourable if there are disjoint sets
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{H}
such that every edge intersects
{L}
and
{R}
.
, and the spectrum of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
is closely related to the hypergraph bipartiteness.

-2
-2
0
1
3
1
Hyperedge
e
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)

Figure 7.3
:
Illustration of

{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
and
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
. Vertices are labelled with their value in
{\bm{f}}_{t}
.

For this reason, we would expect that the diffusion process based on the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
can be used to find sets with small hypergraph bipartiteness.
However, one needs to be very cautious here as, by the nature of the diffusion process, the values
{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)
of all the vertices
v
change over time and, as a result, the sets
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
and
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
that consist of the vertices with the maximum and minimum
{\bm{f}}_{t}
-value
might change after an
infinitesimal
time step; this prevents the process from continuing. We discuss this issue in detail through the so-called
diffusion continuity condition
in Section
7.3.3
.
In essence, the diffusion continuity condition ensures that one can always construct a graph
{G}
by allocating the weight of each hyperedge
e
to the edges in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\times{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
such that the sets
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
and
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
don’t change in infinitesimal time although
{\bm{f}}_{t}
changes according to
(\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t})/(\mathrm{d}t)=-{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t}
.
We also develop an efficient procedure in Section
7.3.3
to compute the weights of edges in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\times{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
. All of these guarantee that (i) every graph that corresponds to the hypergraph diffusion process at any time step can be efficiently constructed;
(ii) with this sequence of constructed graphs, the diffusion process defined by
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
is able to continue until the heat distribution converges.
With this, we can summarise the main idea of the new algorithm as follows:

•

First of all, we introduce some arbitrary
{\bm{f}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the initial diffusion vector, and a step size parameter
\epsilon>0
to discretise
the diffusion process. At each step, the algorithm constructs the graph
{G}
guaranteed by the diffusion continuity condition, and updates
{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
according to the rate of change
(\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t})/(\mathrm{d}t)=-{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t}
. The algorithm terminates when

{\bm{f}}_{t}
has converged, i.e., the ratio between the current Rayleigh quotient
({\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t})/({\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t})

and the one in the previous time step is bounded by some predefined constant.

•

Secondly, similar to many previous spectral graph clustering algorithms (e.g.
[
ACL06
,
TMIY20
,
Tre12
]
),
the algorithm constructs the sweep sets defined by
{\bm{f}}_{t}
and returns the two sets with minimum

{\beta}_{H}
-value among all the constructed sweep sets.
Specifically, for every
i\in[n]
, the algorithm constructs
{L}_{j}=\{v_{i}:\left\lvert{\bm{f}}_{t}(v_{i})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert{\bm{f}}_{t}(v_{j})\right\rvert\land{\bm{f}}_{t}(v_{i})<0\}
and
{R}_{j}=\{v_{i}:\left\lvert{\bm{f}}_{t}(v_{i})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert{\bm{f}}_{t}(v_{j})\right\rvert\land{\bm{f}}_{t}(v_{i})\geq 0\}
.
Then, among the
n
pairs
({L}_{j},{R}_{j})
, the algorithm returns the one with the minimum
{\beta}_{H}
-value.

See Algorithm
1
for the formal description,
and its performance is summarised in Theorem
7.1
.
We prove the theorem in Section
7.5
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Given a hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
and parameter
\epsilon>0
, the following holds:

1.

There is an algorithm that finds an eigen-pair (
\lambda,{\bm{f}}
) of the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
such that
\lambda\leq\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{J}}_{G})
, where
{G}
is the clique reduction of
{H}
and the inequality is strict if
\min_{e\in{E}_{H}}\mathrm{rank}(e)>2
. The algorithm has running time
\mathrm{poly}(\left\lvert{V}_{H}\right\rvert,\left\lvert{E}_{H}\right\rvert,1/\epsilon)
.

2.

Given an eigen-pair
(\lambda,{\bm{f}})
of the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
, there is an algorithm that constructs the two-sided sweep sets defined on
{\bm{f}}
, and finds sets
{L}
and
{R}
such that
{\beta}_{H}({L},{R})\leq\sqrt{2\lambda}
. The algorithm has running time
\mathrm{poly}(\left\lvert{V}_{H}\right\rvert,\left\lvert{E}_{H}\right\rvert)
.

Input :
Hypergraph
{H}
, starting vector
{\bm{f}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, step size
\epsilon>0

Output :
Sets
{L}
and
{R}

t:=0

while

{\bm{f}}_{t}
has not converged

do

1

Use
{\bm{f}}_{t}
to construct graph
{G}
satisfying the diffusion continuity condition

{\bm{f}}_{t+\epsilon}:={\bm{f}}_{t}-\epsilon{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t}

t:=t+\epsilon

end while

2

Set
j:=\argmin_{1\leq i\leq n}{\beta}_{H}({L}_{i},{R}_{i})

return

({L}_{j},{R}_{j})

Algorithm 1

FindBipartiteComponents
(H,{\bm{f}}_{0},\epsilon)

7.3.3
Dealing with the Diffusion Continuity Condition

It remains for us to discuss the diffusion continuity condition, which guarantees that
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
and
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)

don’t change in infinitesimal time and the diffusion process
eventually converges to some stable distribution.
Formally, let
{\bm{f}}_{t}
be the normalised measure on the vertices of
{H}
,
and

{\bm{r}}\triangleq\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}

be the derivative of
{\bm{f}}_{t}
, which describes the rate of change for every vertex at the current time
t
.
We write
{\bm{r}}(v)
for any
v\in{V}_{H}
as

{\bm{r}}(v)=\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)
,
where
{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)
is the contribution of edge
e
towards the rate of change of
{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)
.
Now we discuss three rules that we expect the diffusion process to satisfy, and later prove that these three rules uniquely define the rate of change
{\bm{r}}
.

First of all, as we mentioned in Section
7.3.2
,
we expect that only the vertices in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\cup{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
participate in the diffusion process,
i.e.,
{\bm{r}}_{e}(u)=0
unless
u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\cup{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
. Moreover, any vertex
u
participating in the diffusion process must satisfy the following:

•

Rule (0a): if
\left\lvert{\bm{r}}_{e}(u)\right\rvert>0
and
u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
, then
{\bm{r}}(u)=\max_{v\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)}\{{\bm{r}}(v)\}
.

•

Rule (0b): if
\left\lvert{\bm{r}}_{e}(u)\right\rvert>0
and
u\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
, then
{\bm{r}}(u)=\min_{v\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)}\{{\bm{r}}(v)\}
.

To explain Rule (0), notice that for an infinitesimal time,
{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)
is increased according to
\left(\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}/\mathrm{d}t\right)(u)={\bm{r}}(u)
. Hence, by Rule (0) we know that, if
u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
(resp.
u\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
) participates in the diffusion process in edge
e
, then in an infinitesimal time
{\bm{f}}(u)
remains the maximum (resp. minimum) among the vertices in
e
.
Such a rule is necessary to ensure that the vertices involved in the diffusion in edge
e
do not change in infinitesimal time, and the diffusion process is able to continue.

Our next rule states that the total rate of change of the measure due to edge
e
is equal to
-{w}(e)\cdot{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
:

•

Rule (1):
\sum_{v\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=\sum_{v\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=-{w}(e)\cdot{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
for all
e\in{E}_{H}
.

This rule is a generalisation from the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{G}
in graphs.
In particular, since

{\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)=\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{E}_{G}}{w}_{G}(u,v)({\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+{\bm{f}}_{t}(v))/{d}_{G}(u),

the rate of change of
{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)
due to the edge
\{u,v\}\in{E}_{G}
is
-{w}_{G}(u,v)({\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+{\bm{f}}_{t}(v))/{d}_{G}(u)
.
Rule (1) states that in the hypergraph case the rate of change of the vertices in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
and
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
together behave like the rate of change of
u
and
v
in the graph case.

One might have expected that Rules (0) and (1) together would define a unique process. Unfortunately, this isn’t the case.
For example, let us define an unweighted hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H})
, where
{V}_{H}=\{u,v,w\}
and
{E}_{H}=\{\{u,v,w\}\}
. By setting the measure to be
{\bm{f}}_{t}=(1,1,-2)^{\intercal}
and
e=\{u,v,w\}
, we have
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)=-1
, and
{\bm{r}}(u)+{\bm{r}}(v)={\bm{r}}(w)=1
by Rule (1). In such a scenario, either
\{u,w\}
or
\{v,w\}
can participate in the diffusion and satisfy Rule (0), which makes the process not uniquely defined.
To overcome this, we introduce the following stronger rule to replace Rule (0):

•

Rule (2a): Assume that
\left\lvert{\bm{r}}_{e}(u)\right\rvert>0
and
u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
:

–

if
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)>0
, then
{\bm{r}}(u)=\max_{v\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e))}\{{\bm{r}}(v)\}
;

–

if
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)<0
, then
{\bm{r}}(u)={\bm{r}}(v)
for all
v\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
.

•

Rule (2b): Assume that
\left\lvert{\bm{r}}_{e}(u)\right\rvert>0
and
u\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
:

–

if
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)<0
, then
{\bm{r}}(u)=\min_{v\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e))}\{{\bm{r}}(v)\}
;

–

if
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)>0
, then
{\bm{r}}(u)={\bm{r}}(v)
for all
v\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
.

Notice that the first conditions of Rules (2a) and (2b) correspond to Rules (0a) and (0b) respectively; the second conditions are introduced for purely technical reasons: they state that, if the discrepancy of
e
is negative (resp. positive), then all the vertices
u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
(resp.
u\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
) have the same value of
{\bm{r}}(u)
.
Lemma
7.1
shows that there is a unique
{\bm{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfies Rules (1) and (2), and
{\bm{r}}
can be computed in polynomial time.
Therefore, our two rules uniquely define a diffusion process, and we can use the computed
{\bm{r}}
to simulate the continuous diffusion process with a discretised version.
5
5

5

Note that the graph
{G}
used for the diffusion at time
t
can be easily computed from the
\{{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)\}
values, although in practice this is not actually needed since the
{\bm{r}}(u)
values can be used to update the diffusion directly.

Lemma 7.1

(Existence of diffusion process)
.

For any given
{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there is a unique
{\bm{r}}=\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}/\mathrm{d}t
and associated
\{{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)\}_{e\in{E},v\in{V}}
that satisfy Rule (1) and (2), and
{\bm{r}}
can be computed in polynomial time by linear programming.

It is worth emphasising that
our defined rules and the proof of Lemma
7.1
are more involved than those used in
[
CLTZ18
]
to define the hypergraph Laplacian operator.
In particular, in contrast to
[
CLTZ18
]
, in our case the discrepancy
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
within a hyperedge
e
can be either positive or negative.
This results in the four different cases in Rule (2) which have to be carefully considered throughout the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

7.4
Existence of Diffusion Process

In this section we prove Lemma
7.1
which shows that the diffusion process introduced in Section
7.3
is well-defined.
The proof consists of two parts.
First, we construct a linear program which can compute the rate of change
{\bm{r}}
satisfying the rules of the diffusion process.
Then we analyse the new linear program which establishes Lemma
7.1
.

7.4.1
Computing
{\bm{r}}
by a Linear Program

Now we present an algorithm that computes the vector
{\bm{r}}=\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}/\mathrm{d}t
for any
{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Without loss of generality, let us fix
{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and define a set of equivalence classes
\mathcal{U}
on
{V}
such that vertices
u,v\in{V}_{H}
are in the same equivalence class if
{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)={\bm{f}}_{t}(v)
.
Next we study every equivalence class
{U}\in\mathcal{U}
in turn, and set the
{\bm{r}}
-value of the vertices in
{U}
recursively.
In each iteration, we fix the
{\bm{r}}
-value of some subset
{P}\subseteq{U}
and recurse on
{U}\setminus{P}
.
As we prove later, it’s important to highlight that the recursive procedure ensures that the
{\bm{r}}
-values assigned to the vertices always
decrease
after each recursion.
Notice that it suffices to consider the edges
e
in which
({S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cup{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e))\cap{U}\neq\emptyset
, since the diffusion process induced by other edges
e
has no impact on
{\bm{r}}(u)
for any
u\in{U}
. Hence, we introduce the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{{U}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{e\in{E}_{U}:{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{U}\neq\emptyset\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{U}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{e\in{E}_{U}:{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{U}\neq\emptyset\},

where
{E}_{U}
consists of the edges adjacent to some vertex in
{U}
. To work with the four cases listed in Rule (2a) and (2b), we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{e\in\mathcal{S}_{U}:{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{-}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{e\in\mathcal{S}_{U}:{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)>0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{U}^{+}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{e\in\mathcal{I}_{U}:{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{U}^{-}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{e\in\mathcal{I}_{U}:{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)>0\}.

Our objective is to find some
{P}\subseteq{U}
and assign the same
{\bm{r}}
-value to every vertex in
{P}
.
To this end, for any
{P}\subseteq{U}
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+}:{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\subseteq{P}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{+}:{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\subseteq{P}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{-}:{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{P}\neq\emptyset\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{-}:{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\subseteq{P}\}.

These are the edges contributing to the rate of change of the vertices in
{P}
.
Before continuing the analysis, we briefly explain the intuition behind these four definitions:

1.

For
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
, since every
e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+}
satisfies
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0
and all the vertices in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
must have the same value by Rule (2a), all such
e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
must satisfy that
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\subseteq{P}
, since the unassigned vertices would receive lower values of
{\bm{r}}
in the remaining part of the recursion process.

2.

For
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
, since every
e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{+}
satisfies
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0
, Rule (2b) implies that if
{\bm{r}}_{e}(u)\neq 0
then
{\bm{r}}(u)\leq{\bm{r}}(v)
for all
v\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
. Since unassigned vertices would receive lower values of
{\bm{r}}
later, such
e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
must satisfy
{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\subseteq{P}
.

3.

For
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
, since every
e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{-}
satisfies
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)>0
, by Rule (2a) it suffices that some vertex in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
receives the assignment in the current iteration, i.e., every such
e
must satisfy
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{P}\neq\emptyset
.

4.

The case for
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
is the same as
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
.

As we expect all the vertices
u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
to have the same
{\bm{r}}
-value for every
e
as long as
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0
by Rule (2a) and at the moment we are only considering the assignment of the vertices in
{P}
, we expect that

\left\{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}:{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{P}\neq\emptyset\right\}=\emptyset,

(7.3)

and this ensures that, as long as
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0
and some
u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
gets its
{\bm{r}}
-value, all the other vertices in
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
would be assigned the same value as
u
.
Similarly, by Rule (2b), we expect all the vertices
u\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
to have the same
{\bm{r}}
-value for every
e
as long as
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)>0
, and so we expect that

\left\{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{-}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}:{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{P}\neq\emptyset\right\}=\emptyset.

(7.4)

We set the
{\bm{r}}
-value by dividing the total discrepancy of the edges in
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
between the vertices in
{P}
. As such,
we would like to find some
{P}\subseteq{U}
that maximises the value of

\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({P})}\cdot\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\right).

Taking all of these requirements into account, we show that, for any equivalence class
{U}
, we can find the desired set
{P}
by solving the following linear program:

maximise

\displaystyle c({\bm{x}})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot x_{e}-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot x_{e}

(7.5)

subject to

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in{U}}\begin{aligned} {d}(v)y_{v}&=1\\[12.91663pt]
x_{e}&=y_{u}&\quad e&\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+},u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e),\\
x_{e}&\leq y_{u}&\quad e&\in\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{+},u\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e),\\
x_{e}&\geq y_{u}&\quad e&\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{-},u\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e),\\
x_{e}&=y_{u}&\quad e&\in\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{-},u\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e),\\
x_{e},y_{v}&\geq 0&&\forall v\in{U},e\in{E}_{{U}}.\end{aligned}

Since the linear program only gives partial assignment to the vertices’
{\bm{r}}
-values, we solve the same linear program on the reduced instance given by the set
{U}\setminus{P}
. The formal description of our algorithm is given in Algorithm
2
.

Input :
vertex set
{U}\subseteq{V}
, and edge set
{E}_{{U}}

Output :
Values of
\{{\bm{r}}(v)\}_{v\in{U}}

Construct sets
\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+}
,
\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{-}
,
\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{+}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{-}

Solve the linear program defined by (
7.5
), and define
{P}:=\{v\in{U}:y(v)>0\}

Construct sets
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
,
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
,
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}

Set
C({P}):=c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\right)+c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\right)-c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\right)-c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\right)

Set
\delta({P}):=C({P})/\mathrm{vol}({P})

Set
{\bm{r}}(u):=\delta({P})
for every
u\in{P}

ComputeChangeRate
\left({U}\setminus{P},{E}_{U}\setminus\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\right)\right)

Algorithm 2

ComputeChangeRate
({U},{E}_{U})

7.4.2
Analysis of the Linear Program

Now we analyse Algorithm
2
, and the properties of the
{\bm{r}}
-values it computes.
Specifically, we show the following facts which together allow us to establish Lemma
7.1
.

1.

Algorithm
2
always produces a unique vector
{\bm{r}}
, no matter which optimal result is returned when computing the linear program (
7.5
).

2.

If there is any vector
{\bm{r}}
which is consistent with Rules (1) and (2), then it must be equal to the output of Algorithm
2
.

3.

The vector
{\bm{r}}
produced by Algorithm
2
is consistent with Rules (1) and (2).

Output of Algorithm
2
is Unique

First of all, for any
{P}\subseteq{U}
that satisfies (
7.3
) and (
7.4
), we define vectors
{\bm{x}}_{P}
and
{\bm{y}}_{P}
by

{\bm{x}}_{P}(e)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({P})}&\mbox{if }e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

{\bm{y}}_{P}(v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({P})}&\mbox{if }v\in{P}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

and
z_{P}=\left({\bm{x}}_{P},{\bm{y}}_{P}\right)
.
It is easy to verify that
\left({\bm{x}}_{P},{\bm{y}}_{P}\right)
is a feasible solution to (
7.5
) with the objective value
c\left({\bm{x}}_{P}\right)=\delta({P})
. We prove that
ComputeChangeRate
(Algorithm
2
) computes a unique vector
{\bm{r}}
regardless of how ties are broken when computing the subsets
{P}
.

For any feasible solution
z=({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})
, we say that a non-empty set
{Q}
is a
level set
of
z
if there is some
t>0
such that
{Q}=\left\{u\in{U}:y_{u}\geq t\right\}
. We first show that any non-empty level set of an optimal solution
z
also corresponds to an optimal solution.

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose that
z^{\star}=\left({\bm{x}}^{\star},{\bm{y}}^{\star}\right)
is an optimal solution of the linear program (
7.5
). Then, any non-empty level set
{Q}
of
z^{\star}
corresponds to an optimal solution of (
7.5
) as well.

Proof.

Let
{P}=\{v\in{V}_{H}:{\bm{y}}^{\star}(v)>0\}
.
The proof is by case distinction.
We first look at the case in which all the vertices in
{P}
have the same value of
{\bm{y}}^{\star}(v)
for any
v\in{P}
.
Then, it must be that
z^{\star}=z_{P}
and
every non-empty level set
{Q}
of
z^{\star}
is equal to
{P}
; as such, the statement holds trivially.

Secondly, we assume that the vertices in
{P}
have at least two different
{\bm{y}}^{\star}
-values. We define
\alpha=\min\left\{y_{v}^{\star}:v\in{P}\right\}
, and have

\alpha\cdot\mathrm{vol}({P})<\sum_{u\in{U}}{d}(u)\cdot y_{u}^{\star}=1.

We introduce
\widehat{z}=\left(\widehat{{\bm{x}}},\widehat{{\bm{y}}}\right)
defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{{\bm{x}}}(e)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{{\bm{x}}^{\star}(e)-\alpha}{1-\alpha\mathrm{vol}({P})}&\mbox{if }{\bm{x}}^{\star}(e)\geq 0\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

and

\displaystyle\widehat{{\bm{y}}}(v)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{{\bm{y}}^{\star}(v)-\alpha}{1-\alpha\mathrm{vol}({P})}&\mbox{if }v\in{P}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

This implies that

{\bm{x}}^{\star}=\left(1-\alpha\mathrm{vol}({P})\right)\widehat{{\bm{x}}}+\alpha\cdot{\bm{1}}_{P}=\left(1-\alpha\mathrm{vol}({P})\right)\widehat{{\bm{x}}}+\alpha\cdot\mathrm{vol}({P})\cdot{\bm{x}}_{P},

(7.6)

and

{\bm{y}}^{\star}=\left(1-\alpha\mathrm{vol}({P})\right)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}+\alpha\cdot{\bm{1}}_{P}=\left(1-\alpha\mathrm{vol}({P})\right)\widehat{{\bm{y}}}+\alpha\cdot\mathrm{vol}({P})\cdot{\bm{y}}_{P},

(7.7)

where
{\bm{1}}_{P}
is the indicator vector of the set
{P}
.
Notice that

\widehat{z}
preserves the relative ordering of the vertices and edges with respect to
{\bm{x}}^{\star}
and
{\bm{y}}^{\star}
, and all the constraints in (
7.5
) hold for
\widehat{z}
. These imply that
\widehat{z}
is a feasible solution to (
7.5
) as well.
Moreover, it’s not difficult to see that
\widehat{z}
is an optimal solution of (
7.5
), since otherwise by the linearity of (
7.6
) and (
7.7
)
z_{P}
would have a higher objective value than
z^{\star}
, contradicting the fact that
z^{\star}
is an optimal solution. Hence, the non-empty level set defined by
\widehat{z}
corresponds to an optimal solution.
Finally, by applying the second case inductively, we prove the claimed statement of the lemma.
∎

By applying the lemma above and the linearity of the objective function of (
7.5
), we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 7.1
.

The following statements hold:

•

Suppose that
{P}_{1}
and
{P}_{2}
are optimal subsets of
{U}
. Then,
{P}_{1}\cup{P}_{2}
, as well as
{P}_{1}\cap{P}_{2}
satisfying
{P}_{1}\cap{P}_{2}\neq\emptyset
, is an optimal subset of
{U}
.

•

The optimal set of maximum size is unique, and contains all optimal subsets.

Now we are ready to show that the procedure
ComputeChangeRate
(Algorithm
2
) and the linear program (
7.5
) together always give us the same set of
{\bm{r}}
-values regardless of which optimal solution of (
7.5
) is used for the recursive construction of the entire vector
{\bm{r}}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
({U},{E}_{U})
be the input to
ComputeChangeRate
, and
{P}\subset{U}
be the set returned by (
7.5
). Moreover, let
\left({U}^{\prime}={U}\setminus{P},{E}_{{U}^{\prime}}\right)
be the input to the recursive call
ComputeChangeRate
({U}^{\prime},{E}_{{U}^{\prime}})
. Then, it holds for any
{P}^{\prime}\subseteq{U}^{\prime}
that
\delta({P}^{\prime})\leq\delta({P})
, where the equality holds if and only if
\delta({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})=\delta({P})
.

Proof.

By the definition of the function
c
and sets
\mathcal{S}^{+},\mathcal{S}^{-},\mathcal{I}^{+},\mathcal{I}^{-}
, it holds that

c\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U^{\prime}},{P^{\prime}}}^{+}\right)=c\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P\cup P^{\prime}}}^{+}\right)-c\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\right),

and the same equality holds for sets
\mathcal{S}^{-},\mathcal{I}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
We have that

\displaystyle\delta({P}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{c\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U^{\prime}},{P^{\prime}}}^{+}\right)+c\left(\mathcal{I}_{{U^{\prime}},{P^{\prime}}}^{+}\right)-c\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U^{\prime}},{P^{\prime}}}^{-}\right)-c\left(\mathcal{I}_{{U^{\prime}},{P^{\prime}}}^{-}\right)}{\mathrm{vol}({P}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\left({P}\cup{P}^{\prime}\right)\cdot\mathrm{vol}({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})-\delta({P})\cdot\mathrm{vol}({P})}{\mathrm{vol}({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})-\mathrm{vol}({P})}.

Therefore, it holds for any operator
\bowtie\ \in\{<,=,>\}
that

\displaystyle\delta({P}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\bowtie\delta({P})

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{\delta({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})\cdot\mathrm{vol}({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})-\delta({P})\cdot\mathrm{vol}({P})}{\mathrm{vol}({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})-\mathrm{vol}({P})}

\displaystyle\bowtie\delta({P})

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\delta({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\bowtie\delta({P}),

which implies that
\delta({P}^{\prime})\leq\delta({P})
if and only if
\delta({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})\leq\delta({P})
with equality if and only if
\delta({P}\cup{P}^{\prime})=\delta({P})
.
Since
{P}
is optimal, it cannot be the case that
\delta\left({P}\cup{P}^{\prime}\right)>\delta({P})
, and therefore the lemma follows.
∎

Combining everything together, we have that
for any input instance
({U},{E}_{U})
, Algorithm
2
always returns the same output
{\bm{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\left\lvert{U}\right\rvert}
no matter which optimal sets are returned by solving the linear program (
7.5
). In particular, Algorithm
2
always finds the unique optimal set
{P}\subseteq{U}
of maximum size, and assigns
{\bm{r}}(u)=\delta({P})
to every
u\in{P}
. After removing the computed
{P}\subset{U}
, the computed
{\bm{r}}(v)=\delta({P}^{\prime})
for some
{P}^{\prime}\subseteq{U}\setminus{P}
and any
v\in{P}^{\prime}
is always strictly less than
{\bm{r}}(u)=\delta({P})
for any
u\in{P}
.

Any
{\bm{r}}
Satisfying Rules (1) and (2) is Computed by Algorithm
2

Next we show that if there is any vector
{\bm{r}}
satisfying Rules (1) and (2), it must be equal to the output of Algorithm
2
.

Lemma 7.4
.

For any hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
and
{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if there is a vector
{\bm{r}}=\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}/\mathrm{d}t
with an associated
\{{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)\}_{e\in{E}_{H},v\in{V}_{H}}
satisfying Rules (1) and (2), then
{\bm{r}}
is equal to the output of Algorithm
2
.

Proof.

We focus our attention on a single equivalence class
{U}\subset{V}
where for any
u,v\in{U}
,
{\bm{f}}(u)={\bm{f}}(v)
.
Recall that for each
e\in{E}_{U}
,
c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)={w}(e)\left\lvert{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\right\rvert
, which is the rate of flow due to
e
into
{U}
(if
e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{+}
) or out of
{U}
(if
e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{I}_{{U}}^{-}
).
Let
{\bm{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the vector supposed to satisfy Rules (1) and (2). We assume that
{U}\subseteq{V}
is an arbitrary equivalence class, and define

{T}\triangleq\left\{u\in{U}:{\bm{r}}(u)=\max_{v\in{U}}{\bm{r}}(v)\right\}.

Let us study which properties
{\bm{r}}
must satisfy according to Rules (1) and (2).

•

Assume that
e\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{-}
, i.e., it holds that
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap U\neq\emptyset
and
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)>0
. To satisfy Rule (2a), it suffices to have that

c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)={w}(e)\cdot{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)=-\sum_{v\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=-\sum_{v\in{T}}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)

if
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{T}\neq\emptyset
, and
{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=0
for all
v\in{T}
otherwise.

•

Assume that
e\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{+}
, i.e., it holds that
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{U}\neq\emptyset
and
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0
. To satisfy Rule (2a), it suffices to have
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\subseteq{T}
, or
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cap{T}=\emptyset
.

•

Assume that
e\in\mathcal{I}_{U}^{+}
, i.e., it holds that
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\cap{U}\neq\emptyset
and
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)<0
. To satisfy Rule (2b), it suffices to have that

c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)=\sum_{v\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=\sum_{v\in{T}}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)

if
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\subseteq{T}
, and
{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=0
for all
v\in{T}
otherwise.

•

Assume that
e\in\mathcal{I}_{U}^{-}
, i.e., it holds that
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\cap{U}\neq\emptyset
and
{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)>0
. To satisfy Rule (2b), it suffices to have
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\subseteq{T}
, or
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)\cap{T}=\emptyset
.

Notice that the four conditions above needed to satisfy Rule (2) naturally reflect our definitions of the sets
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+},\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+},\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
and for all
u\in{T}
, it must be that
{\bm{r}}(u)=\delta({T})
.

Now we show that the output set
{P}
returned by solving the linear program (
7.5
)
is the set
{T}
. To prove this, notice that on one hand, by Corollary
7.1
, the linear program gives us the unique maximal optimal subset
{P}\subseteq{U}
, and every
v\in{P}
satisfies that
{\bm{r}}(v)=\delta({P})\leq{\bm{r}}(u)=\delta({T})
for any
u\in{T}
as every vertex in
{T}
has the maximum
{\bm{r}}
-value. On the other side, we have that
\delta({T})\leq\delta({P})
since
{P}
is the set returned by the linear program, and therefore
{T}={P}
. We can apply this argument recursively, and this proves that Algorithm
2
must return the vector
{\bm{r}}
.
∎

Output of Algorithm
2
Satisfies Rules (1) and (2)

Now we show that the output of Algorithm
2
does indeed satisfy Rules (1) and (2) which, together with Lemma
7.4
, implies that there is exactly one such vector satisfying the rules.

Lemma 7.5
.

For any hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
and vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the vector
{\bm{r}}
constructed by Algorithm
2
has corresponding
\{{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)\}_{e\in{E}_{H},v\in{V}_{H}}
which satisfies Rules (1) and (2).
Moreover, the
\{{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)\}_{e\in{E}_{H},v\in{V}_{H}}
values can be computed in polynomial time using the vector
{\bm{r}}
.

Proof.

We focus on a single iteration of the algorithm, in which
r(v)
is assigned for the vertices in some set
{T}\subset{V}_{H}
.
We use the notation

\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}=\mathcal{I}_{{U},{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{T}}^{+},\qquad\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}=\mathcal{I}_{{U},{T}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{T}}^{-}

and show that the values of
{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)
for
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
can be computed and satisfy Rules (1) and (2). Therefore, by applying this argument to each recursive call of the algorithm, we establish the lemma.
Given the set
{T}
, construct the following undirected flow graph, which is illustrated in Figure
7.4
.

•

The vertex set is
\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}\cup{T}\cup\{s,t\}
.

•

For all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}
, there is an edge
(s,e)
with capacity
c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
.

•

For all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
, there is an edge
(e,t)
with capacity
c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
.

•

For all
v\in{T}
, if
\delta({T})\geq 0
, there is an edge
(v,t)
with capacity
{d}(v)\delta({T})
.
Otherwise, there is an edge
(s,v)
with capacity
{d}(v)\left\lvert\delta({T})\right\rvert
.

•

For each
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
, and each
v\in{T}\cap\left({S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cup{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\right)
, there is an edge
(e,v)
with capacity
\infty
.

s
T
t
\mathcal{E}_{T}^{+}
\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-}
\vdots
c(e)
c(e)
d(v)\delta(T)
\infty

(a)

s
T^{\prime}
t
\mathcal{E}_{T^{\prime}}^{+}
\mathcal{E}_{T^{\prime}}^{-}
\vdots
c(e)
c(e)
d(v)\delta(T)
\infty

(b)

Figure 7.4
:

(a)
An illustration of the constructed max-flow graph, when
\delta({T})\geq 0
.
The minimum cut is given by
\{s\}
.
(b)
A cut induced by
{T}^{\prime}\subset{T}
. We can assume that every
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
connected to
{T}^{\prime}
is on the same side of the cut as
{T}^{\prime}
. Otherwise, there would be an edge with infinite capacity crossing the cut.

We use
\mathrm{cut}({A})
to denote the weight of the cut defined by the set
{A}
in this constructed graph, and note that
\mathrm{cut}(\{s\})=\mathrm{cut}(\{t\})
since

\displaystyle\mathrm{cut}(\{s\})-\mathrm{cut}(\{t\})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}}(e)-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}}(e)-\mathrm{vol}({T})\delta({T})=0

by the definition of
\delta({T})
.

Now, suppose that the maximum flow value on this graph is
\mathrm{cut}(\{s\})
, and let the corresponding flow from
u
to
v
be given by
\Theta(u,v)=-\Theta(v,u)
.
Then, set
{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=\Theta(e,v)
for any
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
and
v\in{T}\cap e
.
This configuration of the values
{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)
would be compatible with the vector
{\bm{r}}
computed by Algorithm
2
, and would satisfy the rules of the diffusion process for the following reasons:

•

For all
v\in{T}
, the edge
(v,t)
or
(s,v)
is saturated and so

\sum_{e\in{E}}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)={d}(v)\delta({T})={d}(v){\bm{r}}(v)

.

•

For any
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
, the edge
(s,e)
or
(e,t)
is saturated and so we have

\sum_{v\in{T}\cap e}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{v}(e)=-{w}(e){\Delta}(e).

Since
\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
is removed in the recursive step of the algorithm,
{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=0
for all
v\in{U}\setminus{T}
and so
\sum_{v\in{U}\cap e}{d}(v){\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=-{w}(e){\Delta}(e)
. This establishes Rule (1) since
{U}\cap e
is equal to either
{S}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
or
{I}_{{\bm{f}}}(e)
.

•

For edges in
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{T}}^{+}
(resp.
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{T}}^{+}
,
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{T}}^{-}
), since
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
(resp.
{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
,
{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
) is a subset of
{T}
and every
v\in{T}
has the same value of
{\bm{r}}(v)
, Rule (2) is satisfied.
For edges in
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{T}}^{-}
, for any
v\not\in{T}
, we have
{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)=0
and
{\bm{r}}(v)<\delta({T})
which satisfies Rule (2).

We now show that every cut separating
s
and
t
has weight at least
\mathrm{cut}(\{s\})
which would establish that the maximum flow on this graph is
\mathrm{cut}(\{s\})
by the max-flow min-cut theorem.

Consider some arbitrary cut given by
{X}=\{s\}\cup{T}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{-}
where
{T}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{+}
,
\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{-}
) is a subset of
{T}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}
,
\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
).
Figure
7.4
illustrates this cut.
Since all of the edges not connected to
s
or
t
have infinite capacity, we can assume that no such edge crosses the cut which implies that

•

for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{+}
,
e\cap({T}\setminus{T}^{\prime})=\emptyset
;

•

for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{-}
,
e\cap({T}\setminus{T}^{\prime})=\emptyset
;

•

for all
e\in(\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{+})
,
e\cap{T}^{\prime}=\emptyset
;

•

for all
e\in(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{-})
,
e\cap{T}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

These conditions, along with the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{+}
and
\mathcal{E}_{{T}}^{-}
, allow us to assume that
\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{+}=\mathcal{I}_{{U},{T^{\prime}}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{T^{\prime}}}^{+}
and
\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{-}=\mathcal{I}_{{U},{T^{\prime}}}^{-}\cup\mathcal{S}_{{U},{T^{\prime}}}^{-}
.
The size of this arbitrary cut is

\mathrm{cut}({X})=\mathrm{cut}(\{s\})-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{+}}c(e)+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{-}}c(e)+\sum_{v\in{T}^{\prime}}{d}(v)\delta({T}).

Since
{T}
maximises the objective function
\delta
, we have

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{+}}c(e)-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{{T}^{\prime}}^{-}}c(e)=\mathrm{vol}({T}^{\prime})\delta({T}^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{vol}({T}^{\prime})\delta({T})=\sum_{v\in{T}^{\prime}}{d}(v)\delta({T})

and can conclude that
\mathrm{cut}({X})\geq\mathrm{cut}(\{s\})
. This completes the proof.
∎

We can now combine the results in Lemmas
7.4
and
7.5
to prove Lemma
7.1
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Lemma
7.4
and Lemma
7.5
together imply that there is a unique vector
{\bm{r}}
and corresponding
\{{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)\}_{e\in{E}_{H},v\in{V}_{H}}
which satisfies Rules (1) and (2).
Lemma
7.4
further shows that Algorithm
2
computes this vector
{\bm{r}}
, and the proof of Lemma
7.5
gives a polynomial-time algorithm for computing the
\{{\bm{r}}_{e}(v)\}
values by solving a sequence of max-flow problems.
∎

7.5
Analysis of Algorithm
1

In this section we
analyse the
FindBipartiteComponents
algorithm and
prove Theorem
7.1
.
This section consists of two parts corresponding to the two statements in Theorem
7.1
.
First, we show that the diffusion process converges to an eigenvector of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
.
We then show that this allows us to find sets
{L},{R}\subset{V}_{H}
with low hypergraph bipartiteness.

7.5.1
Convergence of the Diffusion Process

We now show that the diffusion process determined by the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
converges in polynomial time to an eigenvector of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
.

Theorem 7.2
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there is some
t=O\!\left(1/\epsilon^{3}\right)
such that for any starting vector
{\bm{f}}_{0}
, there is an interval
[c,c+2\epsilon]
such that

\frac{1}{\left\|{\bm{f}}_{t}\right\|_{w}}\sum_{{\bm{u}}_{i}:\lambda_{i}\in[c,c+2\epsilon]}\left\langle{\bm{f}}_{t},{\bm{u}}_{i}\right\rangle_{w}\geq 1-\epsilon,

where
({\bm{u}}_{i},\lambda_{i})
are the eigen-pairs of
{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{t}={\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}({\mathbf{D}}_{{G}_{t}}+{\mathbf{A}}_{{G}_{t}})
and
{G}_{t}
is the graph constructed to represent the diffusion operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
at time
t
.

By taking
\epsilon
to be some small constant, this shows that the vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
converges to an eigenvector of the hypergraph operator in polynomial time.

Proof.

We show that the Rayleigh quotient
R_{{\mathbf{J}}_{H}}({\bm{f}}_{t})
is always decreasing at a rate of at least
\epsilon^{3}
whenever the conclusion of Theorem
7.2
does not hold. Since
R_{{\mathbf{J}}_{H}}({\bm{f}}_{t})
can only decrease by a constant amount, the theorem follows.

First, we derive an expression for the rate of change of the Rayleigh quotient
R_{{\mathbf{J}}_{H}}({\bm{f}}_{t})
.
Let
{\bm{x}}_{t}\triangleq{\mathbf{D}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}
, and

{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}={\mathbf{D}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{H}({\mathbf{D}}_{{G}_{t}}+{\mathbf{A}}_{{G}_{t}}){\mathbf{D}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{H}.

Then, we have

R_{{\mathbf{J}}_{H}}({\bm{f}}_{t})=\frac{{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}({\mathbf{D}}_{{G}_{t}}+{\mathbf{A}}_{{G}_{t}}){\bm{f}}_{t}}{{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}}=\frac{{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t}}{{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}_{t}}.

For every eigenvector
{\bm{u}}_{j}
of
{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
, the vector
{\bm{v}}_{j}={\mathbf{D}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{H}{\bm{u}}_{j}
is an eigenvector of
{\mathbf{Z}}
with the same eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
.
Since
{\mathbf{Z}}
is symmetric, the vectors
{\bm{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{v}}_{n}
are orthogonal.
Additionally, notice that

\left\langle{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{v}}_{j}\right\rangle={\bm{f}}^{\intercal}_{t}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{u}}_{j}=\left\langle{\bm{f}}_{t},{\bm{u}}_{j}\right\rangle_{w}

and we define
\alpha_{j}=\left\langle{\bm{f}}_{t},{\bm{u}}_{j}\right\rangle_{w}
so we can write
{\bm{x}}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}{\bm{v}}_{j}
and

{\bm{f}}_{t}={\mathbf{D}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{H}{\bm{x}}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}{\bm{u}}_{j}.

Now, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\langle{\bm{x}}_{t},{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\bm{x}}_{t},{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t}\right\rangle+\left\langle{\bm{x}}_{t},\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{2}{\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{2}{\bm{x}}_{t}

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}^{2}.

Additionally, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}_{t}=-{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t}=-2{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t}.

Recalling that
{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}
, this gives

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}R({\bm{f}}_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{({\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}_{t})^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}({\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t})\right)({\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}_{t})-\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}({\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}_{t})\right)({\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\bm{x}}_{t}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}({\bm{x}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}{\bm{x}}_{t})\right)+2R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2}

\displaystyle=2\left(R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2}-\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}^{2}\right)

(7.8)

We now show that at any time
t
, if the conclusion of the theorem does not hold, then

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}R({\bm{f}}_{t})\leq-\epsilon^{3}.

(7.9)

Assuming that this holds and using the fact that
R({\bm{f}}_{0})\leq 2
, when
t=2/\epsilon^{3}
, either
R({\bm{f}}_{t})=0
or there is some
t^{\prime}<t
when
(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t^{\prime})R({\bm{f}}_{t^{\prime}})>-\epsilon^{3}
and the conclusion of the theorem holds.

Now, to show (
7.9
), consider the partial derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{i}}2\left(R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2}-\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{2\alpha_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\frac{2\alpha_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\lambda_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\alpha_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}(R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\lambda_{i}),

(7.10)

where we use the fact that
R({\bm{f}}_{t})=(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j})/(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2})
.
Notice that
the derivative in (
7.10
) is greater than
0

if
\lambda_{i}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})
,
and
the derivative is less than
0

if
\lambda_{i}>R({\bm{f}}_{t})
.
This means that, in order to establish an upper-bound of
(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t)R({\bm{f}}_{t})
, we can assume that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}
are as close to the value of
R({\bm{f}}_{t})
as possible.

Now, we assume that at time
t
the conclusion of the theorem does not hold. Then, one of the following cases must hold:

1.

(\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}>R({\bm{f}}_{t})+\epsilon}\alpha_{j}^{2})/(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2})>\epsilon/2
;

2.

(\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\epsilon}\alpha_{j}^{2})/(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2})>\epsilon/2
.

Suppose the first case holds.
By the conclusions we draw from (
7.10
), we can assume that there is an eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}=R({\bm{f}}_{t})+\epsilon
such that
\alpha_{i}^{2}/(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2})=\epsilon/2
and that

\left(\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2}\right)\Bigg/\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\right)=1-\epsilon/2.

Then, since

R({\bm{f}}_{t})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}\right)\Bigg/\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\right),

we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}=R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}(R({\bm{f}}_{t})+\epsilon),

which is equivalent to

\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t}))=\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot(R({\bm{f}}_{t})+\epsilon)=-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}.

(7.11)

Now, notice that for any
\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})
we have

\displaystyle(\lambda_{j}^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2})

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{j}+R({\bm{f}}_{t}))(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t}))

\displaystyle\geq 2R({\bm{f}}_{t})(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})),

since
\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})<0
.
As such, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}R({\bm{f}}_{t})

\displaystyle=2\left(R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2}-\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2})

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\left((R({\bm{f}}_{t})+\epsilon)^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2}\right)-\frac{2}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2})

\displaystyle\leq-\epsilon\left(2\epsilon R({\bm{f}}_{t})+\epsilon^{2}\right)-\frac{2}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})}2\alpha_{j}^{2}R({\bm{f}}_{t})(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t}))

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon^{2}R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\epsilon^{3}+2\epsilon^{2}R({\bm{f}}_{t})

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{3},

where the second last equality follows by (
7.11
).
We now consider the second case.
We assume that there is an eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}=R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\epsilon
such that
\alpha_{i}^{2}/(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2})=\epsilon/2
, and that
(\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}>R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2})/(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2})=1-\epsilon/2
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}>R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}=R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\left(R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\epsilon\right),

which is equivalent to

\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}>R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t}))=\frac{\epsilon}{2}R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}(R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\epsilon)=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}.

(7.12)

Now, notice that for any
\lambda_{j}>R({\bm{f}}_{t})
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2}

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{j}+R({\bm{f}}_{t}))(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t}))

\displaystyle\geq 2R({\bm{f}}_{t})\cdot(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})).

As such, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}R({\bm{f}}_{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2})

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\left((R({\bm{f}}_{t})-\epsilon)^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2}\right)-\frac{2}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}>R({\bm{f}}_{t})}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}^{2}-R({\bm{f}}_{t})^{2})

\displaystyle\leq-\epsilon\left(\epsilon^{2}-2\epsilon R({\bm{f}}_{t})\right)-\frac{2}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}<R({\bm{f}}_{t})}2\alpha_{j}^{2}R({\bm{f}}_{t})(\lambda_{j}-R({\bm{f}}_{t}))

\displaystyle=2\epsilon^{2}g(t)-\epsilon^{3}-2\epsilon^{2}R({\bm{f}}_{t})

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{3}

where the second last equality follows by (
7.12
).
These two cases establish (
7.9
) and complete the proof of the theorem.
∎

Relationship of the Eigenvalue to the Clique Graph

We now show that the eigenvalue corresponding to the eigenvector to which the algorithm converges is at most the minimum eigenvalue of
{\mathbf{J}}_{G}
where
{G}
is the clique reduction of
{H}
.
We start by showing the following technical lemma.

Lemma 7.6
.

For any hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
, vector
{\bm{f}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and edge
e\in{E}_{H}
, it holds that

\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{u,v\in e}\frac{1}{\mathrm{rank}(e)-1}({\bm{f}}(u)+{\bm{f}}(v))^{2}.

The equality holds if and only if there is exactly one vertex
v\in e
with
{\bm{f}}(v)\neq 0
or
\mathrm{rank}(e)=2
.

Proof.

We consider some ordering of the vertices in
e
,

u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{\mathrm{rank}(e)},

such that
u_{1}=\argmax_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)
and
u_{2}=\argmin_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)
and the remaining vertices are ordered arbitrarily.
Then, for any
2\leq k\leq\mathrm{rank}(e)
, we define

C_{k}=\sum_{u,v\in\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}}\frac{1}{k-1}({\bm{f}}(u)+{\bm{f}}(v))^{2}.

We show by induction on
k
that

C_{k}\geq\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)^{2}

(7.13)

for all
2\leq k\leq\mathrm{rank}(e)
, with equality if and only if
k=2
or there is exactly one vertex
u_{i}\in e
with
{\bm{f}}(v)\neq 0
.
The lemma follows by setting
k=\mathrm{rank}(e)
.

The base case of
k=2
follows trivially by the definitions and the choice of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.

For the inductive step, we assume that (
7.13
) holds for some
k
, and show that it holds for
k+1
.
We have that

C_{k+1}=\sum_{u,v\in\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k+1}\}}\frac{1}{k}\cdot({\bm{f}}(u)+{\bm{f}}(v))^{2},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle C_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}({\bm{f}}(u_{i})+{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1}))^{2}+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{u,v\in\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}}({\bm{f}}(u)+{\bm{f}}(v))^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}({\bm{f}}(u_{i})+{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1}))^{2}+\frac{k-1}{k}C_{k}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}({\bm{f}}(u_{1})+{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1}))^{2}

where the final inequality holds by the induction hypothesis.
Therefore, it is sufficient to show that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left({\bm{f}}(u_{i})+{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\right)^{2}\geq\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)^{2}.

We instead show the stronger fact that

\left(\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)+{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\right)^{2}+\left(\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)+{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\right)^{2}\geq\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)^{2}.

(7.14)

The proof is by case distinction.
The first case is when

\mathrm{sign}(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(v))=\mathrm{sign}(\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)).

Assume without loss of generality that the sign is positive.
Then, since
{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\geq\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)
, we have

\left(\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)+{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\right)^{2}\geq\left(\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)+\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)^{2}

and (
7.14
) holds.
Moreover, the inequality is strict if
\left\lvert\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right\rvert>0
or
\left\lvert{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\right\rvert>0
.

The second case is when

\mathrm{sign}(\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u))\neq\mathrm{sign}(\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)).

Expanding (
7.14
), we would like to show

\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)^{2}+\left(\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)^{2}+2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)+2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\left(\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)\\
+2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})^{2}\geq\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)^{2}+\left(\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)^{2}-2\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)\left\lvert\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right\rvert,

which is equivalent to

2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})^{2}+2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)+2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\left(\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)\geq-2\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)\left\lvert\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right\rvert.

Notice that exactly one of the terms on the left hand side is negative.
Recalling that
\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\leq{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\leq\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)
, it is clear that

•

if
{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})<0
, then

-2\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)\left\lvert\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right\rvert\leq 2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\left(\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)\leq 0

and the inequality holds.

•

if
{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\geq 0
, then

-2\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)\left\lvert\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right\rvert\leq 2{\bm{f}}(u_{k+1})\left(\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)\leq 0,

and the inequality holds.

Moreover, in both cases the inequality is strict if
-2\left(\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)\right)\left\lvert\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right\rvert<0
or
\left\lvert{\bm{f}}(u_{k_{1}})\right\rvert>0
.
∎

Now, we show that one always finds an eigenvector whose corresponding eigenvalue is at most the minimum eigenvalue of the clique reduction.

Lemma 7.7
.

For any hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
with clique reduction
{G}
, if
{\bm{f}}
is the eigenvector corresponding to
\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G})
, then

\frac{{\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}}{{\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{f}}}\leq\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G})

and the inequality is strict if
\min_{e\in{E}_{H}}\mathrm{rank}(e)>2
.

Proof.

Since
\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G})=({\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}})/({\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{G}{\bm{f}})
and
({\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{G}{\bm{f}})=({\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{f}})
by the construction of the clique graph, it suffices to show that

{\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}\leq{\bm{f}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{G}{\bm{f}}.

This is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}{w}(e)\left(\max_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}(v)+\min_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}(u)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(u,v)\in{E}_{G}}{w}_{G}(u,v)({\bm{f}}(u)+{\bm{f}}(v))^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}{w}(e)\sum_{u,v\in e}\frac{1}{\mathrm{rank}(e)-1}({\bm{f}}(u)+{\bm{f}}(v))^{2}

which holds by Lemma
7.6
.

Furthermore, if
\min_{e\in{E}_{H}}\mathrm{rank}(e)>2
, then by Lemma
7.6
the inequality is strict unless every edge
e\in{E}_{H}
contains at most one
v\in e
with
{\bm{f}}(v)\neq 0
.
Suppose the inequality is not strict, then it must be that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{(u,v)\in{E}_{G}}{w}_{G}(u,v)({\bm{f}}(v)+{\bm{f}}(u))^{2}}{\sum_{v\in{V}_{G}}{d}_{G}(v){\bm{f}}(v)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{v\in{V}_{G}}{d}_{G}(v){\bm{f}}(v)^{2}}{\sum_{v\in{V}_{G}}{d}_{G}(v){\bm{f}}(v)^{2}}

\displaystyle=1,

since for every edge
(u,v)\in{E}_{G}
, at most one of
{\bm{f}}(u)
or
{\bm{f}}(v)
is not equal to
0
.
This cannot be the case, since it is well-known that the maximum eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G})=2
and so
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G})\geq(n-1)+2=n+1
which contradicts the fact that the trace
\mathrm{tr}({\mathbf{D}}_{G}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{G})
is equal to
n
. This proves the final statement of the lemma.
∎

7.5.2
Cheeger-type Inequality for Hypergraph Bipartiteness

We now prove some intermediate facts about the new hypergraph operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
.
These allow us to show that the operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
has a well-defined minimum eigenvector, and the corresponding eigenvalue satisfies a Cheeger-type inequality for hypergraph bipartiteness.
Given a hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
, any edge
e\in{E}_{H}
and weighted measure vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
, let
r_{e}^{{S}}\triangleq\max_{v\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)}\{{\bm{r}}(v)\}
and
r_{e}^{{I}}\triangleq\min_{v\in{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)}\{{\bm{r}}(v)\}
; recall that
c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)={w}(e)\left\lvert{\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\right\rvert
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Given a hypergraph
{H}=({V}_{H},{E}_{H},{w})
and normalised measure vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
, let

{\bm{r}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{f}}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}.

Then, it holds that

\left\|{\bm{r}}\right\|_{w}^{2}=-\sum_{e\in{E}}{w}(e){\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\left(r_{e}^{{S}}+r_{e}^{{I}}\right).

Proof.

Let
{P}\subset{V}
be one of the densest vertex sets defined with respect to the solution of the linear program (
7.5
). By the description of Algorithm
2
, we have
{\bm{r}}(u)=\delta({P})
for every
u\in{P}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in{P}}{d}(u){\bm{r}}(u)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{vol}({P})\cdot\delta({P})^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\right)+c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}\right)-c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\right)-c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}\right)\right)\cdot\delta({P})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\right)\cdot\delta({P})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{{S}}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{{I}}-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{{S}}-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{{I}}.

Since each vertex is included in exactly one set
{P}
and each edge appears either in one each of
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}
or in one each of
\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
and
\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|{\bm{r}}\|^{2}_{w}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in{V}}{d}(v){\bm{r}}(v)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{P}}\sum_{v\in{P}}{d}(v){\bm{r}}(v)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{P}}\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{S}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{+}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{{I}}-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{S}_{{U},{P}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{S}-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{I}_{{U},{P}}^{-}}c_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\cdot r_{e}^{{I}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{e\in{E}}{w}(e){\Delta}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\left(r_{e}^{S}+r_{e}^{{I}}\right),

which proves the lemma.
∎

Next, we define
\gamma_{1}=\min_{\bm{f}}D({\bm{f}})
and show that any vector
{\bm{f}}
that satisfies
\gamma_{1}=D({\bm{f}})
is an eigenvector of
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
with eigenvalue
\gamma_{1}
.
We start by showing that the Raleigh quotient of the new operator is equivalent to the discrepancy ratio of the hypergraph.

Lemma 7.9
.

For any hypergraph
{H}
and vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds that
D({\bm{f}}_{t})=R_{{\mathbf{J}}_{H}}({\bm{f}}_{t})
.

Proof.

Since
{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}=\sum_{v\in{V}}{d}(v){\bm{f}}_{t}(v)^{2}
, it is sufficient to show that

{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}=\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}{w}(e)\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)\right)^{2}.

Recall that for some graph
{G}_{t}
,
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}={\mathbf{D}}_{{G}_{t}}+{\mathbf{A}}_{{G}_{t}}
.
Then, we have that

\displaystyle{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}

\displaystyle={\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}({\mathbf{D}}_{{G}_{t}}+{\mathbf{A}}_{{G}_{t}}){\bm{f}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(u,v)\in{E}_{G}}{w}_{G}(u,v)({\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+{\bm{f}}_{t}(v))^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}\left(\sum_{(u,v)\in{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\times{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)}{w}_{{G}_{t}}(u,v)({\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+{\bm{f}}_{t}(v))^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in{E}_{H}}{w}(e)\left(\max_{u\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(u)+\min_{v\in e}{\bm{f}}_{t}(v)\right)^{2},

which follows since the graph
{G}_{t}
is constructed by splitting the weight of each hyperedge
e\in{E}_{H}
between the edges
{S}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)\times{I}_{{\bm{f}}_{t}}(e)
.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

For a hypergraph
{H}
, operator
{\mathbf{J}}_{H}
, and vector
{\bm{f}}_{t}
, the following statements hold:

1.

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\bm{f}}_{t}\right\|_{w}^{2}=-2{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}
;

2.

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}({\bm{f}}_{t}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t})=-2\left\|{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}\right\|_{w}^{2}
;

3.

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}R({\bm{f}}_{t})\leq 0
with equality if and only if
{\mathbf{D}}_{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{H}{\bm{f}}_{t}\in\mathrm{span}({\bm{f}}_{t})
.

Proof.

By definition, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\left\|{\bm{f}}_{t}\right\|_{w}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sum_{v\in{V}}{d}(v){\bm{f}}_{t}(v)^{2}
